```yaml
textual_basis: At 126b-126c the visitors identify Antiphon as the person to ask because he had often heard the arguments from Pythodorus, a companion of Zeno, and had memorized them carefully when younger.
```

```yaml
  The reported account is routed through named transmitters: Pythodorus told the arguments to Antiphon, and Antiphon is then asked to recount them to the visitors.
textual_basis: |
  At 126c the frame explains that Antiphon had often heard the arguments from Pythodorus and memorized them as a youth. At 127a the visitors ask Antiphon to go through the arguments, and he agrees to narrate after first noting the size of the task.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0005

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Antiphon is identified prosopographically through his grandfather and namesake: he now spends most of his time on horsemanship like his grandfather, after having memorized the arguments as a youth. The detail about the grandfather links Antiphon to an identifiable Athenian family line.
textual_basis: |
  At 126c, the speaker reports that Antiphon as a youth memorized the arguments well, but now spends most of his time on horsemanship after his grandfather and namesake. At 127a, Antiphon is found at home handing over a bridle to a smith to be prepared, which visually confirms the horsemanship identification.
limits: |
  This observation identifies the prosopographic tagging technique but does not claim that the historical Antiphon is independently attested or that the detail carries dramatic irony. The segment supplies the identification without developing it further.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: |
  Antiphon initially resists the request to recount the arguments, citing that it is a considerable work, before eventually agreeing to narrate. This creates a turn-taking friction at the outermost frame level before the reported dialogue even begins.
textual_basis: |
  At 127a, after the visitors explain their purpose, Antiphon recognizes the narrator from a previous visit and greets him. When asked to go through the arguments, he first hesitates because it is a great task, and then nevertheless proceeds to narrate.
limits: |
  This observation notes a turn-taking pattern at the frame level. It does not claim that Antiphon's reluctance signals unreliability or that the reported dialogue is distorted. The segment cuts off before the inner dialogue begins.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 127b the text gives Parmenides' age (about 65), hair color (quite gray), and appearance (noble and good). Zeno is given an age (nearly 40), height (tall), and appearance (graceful), with the added report that he was Parmenides' beloved.
limits: The relationship claim is hedged as something "said" or reported rather than directly asserted; the passage does not specify who reported it or on what authority.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 127d, Pythodorus is named as the one who went out to meet Parmenides and Aristoteles (ton ton triakonta genomenon). The passage identifies Aristoteles by his later role among the Thirty Tyrants, anchoring him as a known historical figure. Zeno is also named as the source of prior reports.
limits: This record only notes the named cast at the report's origin. It does not analyze the frame architecture, the significance of Aristoteles's later career, or the dialogue's dramatic date.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 127d, the narrator (identified through Pythodorus's own report, autos te epeiselthein ephe ho Pythodoros) establishes a chain of transmission with limited direct access to the written text. The phrase smikr' atta indicates partial hearing, and the prior acquaintance with Zeno's account adds another layer.
limits: This observation identifies the embedded report structure but does not evaluate its reliability implications or compare it to other Platonic reported frames.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0012
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 127d-127e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  text_sha256: cd341206d0b348ffb31403f633f960ee950647943ae4e7d417d57cb221804995
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates interrupts the reading of Zeno's text by requesting that the first hypothesis of the first argument be read again, and immediately upon its re-reading (127e) poses a direct question to Zeno about its meaning.
textual_basis: At 127d-e, Socrates (ton Sokrate) hears the reading and commands that the first hypothesis be read again (palin te keleusai ten proten hupothesin tou protou logou anagnonai). Upon re-reading, Socrates immediately questions Zeno (pos, phanai, o Zenon, touto legeis;), seizing control of the exchange from the written text to live interrogation.
limits: This record only notes the interruption and question structure. It does not analyze the content of the hypothesis or whether Socrates's question is adversarial.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0014
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 128e-129a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 128e-129a
  start_marker: 128e
  end_marker: 129a
  start_char: 5004
  end_char: 5761
  text_sha256: e46e36781b5bc2390d22624b901e5f9bd04b74de370dfeb474c14ec0271cbd8a
greek_terms:
  - εἶδός τι ὁμοιότητος
  - ὃ ἔστιν ἀνόμοιον
english_gloss: a form of likeness ... what is unlike
observation: Socrates asks whether there is a form of likeness itself by itself (auto kath' hauto eidos ti homoiotetos) and a contrary form, the unlike (anomoion), framing these as distinct self-subsistent entities in which particular things participate.
textual_basis: At 128e-129a, Socrates explicitly names a form of likeness existing auto kath' hauto and its contrary, the unlike, and asks whether particular things come to be like or unlike by participating (metalambanein) in these forms.
limits: This records Socrates' formulation of forms and participation. It does not assess whether Parmenides will later challenge this picture or whether the formulation is internally consistent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0015
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 128e-129a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 128e-129a
  start_marker: 128e
  end_marker: 129a
  start_char: 5004
  end_char: 5761
  text_sha256: e46e36781b5bc2390d22624b901e5f9bd04b74de370dfeb474c14ec0271cbd8a
greek_terms:
  - μεταλαμβάνειν
english_gloss: participate ... the many
observation: "Socrates describes participation in forms as admitting of degree: things become like to the extent that (kata tosouton hoson) they participate in likeness, and things can participate in both contraries and thus be both like and unlike."
textual_basis: At 129a, Socrates says things become like 'in that respect and to the extent that' (tautei te kai kata tosouton hoson) they participate, and adds that all things may participate in both contraries (amphoin) and thus be both like and unlike.
limits: This records the graded participation language attributed to the young Socrates. It does not evaluate whether the mature Plato endorses this formulation or whether the Third Man argument undermines it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0016
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 128c-128d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 128c-128d
  start_marker: 128c
  end_marker: 128d
  start_char: 4157
  end_char: 5004
  text_sha256: e696f0837c7d8f2c3c1351d51ef592aeeb654cbaa186239a2955bd321bd688bb
greek_terms:
  - ἀνταποδίδωσι
  - φιλονικίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Zeno characterizes his written work as an antapodosis — a counter-reply that returns the same charges and more (tauta kai pleio) against opponents. The work is described as arising from a spirit of philonikia (love of victory, contentious rivalry).
textual_basis: At 128d, Zeno says the writing 'antapodidōsi tauta kai pleiō' (returns the same things and more) against those who assert plurality. He then attributes the work's composition to 'toiautēn dē philonikian' (such a contentious rivalry), locating the work in an agonistic exchange rather than a purely investigative context.
limits: This observation notes the agonistic framing Zeno gives his work but does not determine whether the arguments themselves are merely eristic or have genuine philosophical content. The term philonikia could be self-deprecating or merely descriptive of the polemical genre.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0017
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 128c-128d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 128c-128d
  start_marker: 128c
  end_marker: 128d
  start_char: 4157
  end_char: 5004
  text_sha256: e696f0837c7d8f2c3c1351d51ef592aeeb654cbaa186239a2955bd321bd688bb
greek_terms:
  - ὑπὸ νέου ὄντος
  - ἔκλεψε
english_gloss: ""
observation: Zeno states that the written work was composed when he was young (hypo neou ontos) and that someone stole the written copy (tis auto eklepse grapsen), with the result that he never had the opportunity to deliberate about whether it should be made public.
textual_basis: At 128d, Zeno says the work was written 'hypo neou ontos emou' (by me when I was young), and 'kai tis auto eklepse grapsen' (and someone stole it once written), so that 'oude bouleusasthai exegeneto' (it was not even possible to deliberate) whether it should be brought out.
limits: This observation records Zeno's autobiographical claim about the composition's circumstances. It does not adjudicate whether the story of the theft is literally true or a dramatic device to distance the mature Zeno from a youthful work.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0018
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 129b-129c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 129b-129c
  start_marker: 129b
  end_marker: 129c
  start_char: 5761
  end_char: 6603
  text_sha256: 4c03f6f0723eba9f75c6eb3bc9f9c1e36d85bc07a9cdb8d22ebcb77cf989dba8
greek_terms:
  - γένη
  - εἴδη
english_gloss: genera and species themselves undergoing opposite affections
observation: Socrates marks a distinction between sensible participants exhibiting opposite properties and the Forms themselves undergoing opposite affections, and says that only the latter would truly deserve wonder.
textual_basis: At 129b-c Socrates uses the phrase "the kinds and forms themselves" (auta ta gene te kai eide) and says that if someone showed these undergoing opposite affections in themselves that would be worthy of wonder, whereas showing a sensible particular like himself as both one and many is unsurprising because sensible particulars participate in opposite Forms.
limits: This observation notes the contrast Socrates draws but does not assess whether the contrast is sustainable under Parmenides' subsequent critique. It does not claim that Socrates here posits a full separation theory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0019
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 129b-129c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 129b-129c
  start_marker: 129b
  end_marker: 129c
  start_char: 5761
  end_char: 6603
  text_sha256: 4c03f6f0723eba9f75c6eb3bc9f9c1e36d85bc07a9cdb8d22ebcb77cf989dba8
greek_terms:
  - μετέχειν τοῦ ἑνὸς
  - μετέχω
english_gloss: participating in the one, participating in plurality
observation: "Socrates uses participation language to explain how the same sensible things can be both one and many: they participate in the one and also participate in plurality. He also applies this to himself as a sensible particular."
textual_basis: At 129b Socrates says someone might show all things are one by participating in the one (to metechein tou henos) and the same things are many by participating in plurality (to plethous metechein). At 129c, explaining how he himself can be both one and many, he says "for I think I participate in plurality" (pletous gar oimai metecho).
limits: This observation records the participation explanation as it appears in Socrates' speech. It does not adjudicate whether this is a consistent theory of participation or how it relates to the Forms' own nature.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0021
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 130c-130d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 130c-130d
  start_marker: 130c
  end_marker: 130d
  start_char: 8295
  end_char: 9182
  text_sha256: 875c901bc879c255cac2fe945dfe0cbd83151c6e6bd5ab6d1467f5606aa4f355
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates hesitates to posit separate forms for base or trivial things such as hair, mud, and dirt, treating them as a class apart from the forms he typically accepts.
textual_basis: Parmenides asks at 130c whether Socrates is perplexed (aporia) about whether there is a separate form for hair, mud, dirt, or anything most dishonored and trivial (atimotaton te kai phaulotaton). At 130d Socrates answers that the things we see simply are what they are, and that to suppose a form of such things would be too strange (me lian e atopon). He adds that this thought has sometimes troubled him, fearing he might fall into a bottomless pit of nonsense (buthon phluarias).
limits: This observation records Socrates' selective application of forms language; it does not prove that Plato's own theory excludes such forms, nor does it address whether later dialogues reconsider them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0022
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 130c-130d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 130c-130d
  start_marker: 130c
  end_marker: 130d
  start_char: 8295
  end_char: 9182
  text_sha256: 875c901bc879c255cac2fe945dfe0cbd83151c6e6bd5ab6d1467f5606aa4f355
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates admits to fear-driven avoidance: when he confronts the possibility that there might be forms of everything, he flees for fear of falling into a depth of nonsense (buthon phluarias) and retreats to safer forms."
textual_basis: At 130d Socrates says that the thought about forms of trivial things has sometimes troubled him (me ti e peri panton tauton), and when he takes that stand he flees (pheugon oichomai), fearing that he might fall into some depth of nonsense and be destroyed (deisas me pote eis tina buthon phluarias empeson diaphtaro). He explicitly says he returns to the forms they were just discussing and passes his time occupied with those.
limits: This is a confession of psychological avoidance, not a refutation. It does not establish whether Socrates' fear is epistemically warranted or whether Parmenides will later force him to confront this difficulty.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - ταὐτὸν
  - τρόπον τινὰ
  - μεταβάλλων
  - ἐξαπατᾶν
  - ἕτερόν τι λέγων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates claims Zeno's treatise says the same thing as Parmenides' poem but Zeno tries to deceive the audience into thinking it says something different by changing the form (metaballon), even though the content is nearly identical (schedon ti legontas tauta).
textual_basis: "At 128a-128b Socrates tells Parmenides that Zeno has written 'the same thing in a certain way as you' (tauton gar gegraphe tropon tina hoper su), but by changing it (metaballon) he tries to deceive us (exapatan) into thinking he says something different (heteron ti legon). Socrates then explains the parallel: Parmenides says 'one is,' Zeno says 'not many,' and each speaks so as to appear to say nothing the same (meden ton auton eirekenai dokein) while saying nearly the same things (schedon ti legontas tauta). Socrates is not accusing himself of irony; he is detecting what he presents as a deceptive strategy in Zeno's writing, framed as a polite observation to Parmenides."
limits: This observation records Socrates' claim about Zeno's rhetorical strategy, not the truth of that claim. It does not establish that Zeno actually intended deception. Socrates is marking what he takes to be a kind of dissimulation but it is an accusation of deception by formal variation rather than the typical Socratic profession of ignorance.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - ποιήμασιν
  - γράμμα
  - συγγράμματι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The opening exchange references two written works: Parmenides' poem (en tois poiemasin) and Zeno's treatise (to gramma, to sungrammati). Socrates references both as existing texts with identifiable theses — Parmenides' 'one is' (hen pheis einai to pan) and Zeno's 'not many' (ou polla phesin einai) — and contrasts the evidence (tekmeria) each author provides."
textual_basis: At 128a Socrates says he understands Zeno's treatise (holon to gramma ho bouletai). At 128a-128b he refers to Parmenides' poems (en tois poiemasin) and notes that Zeno wishes to be associated with Parmenides not only through friendship but through his writing (to sungrammati). Socrates then contrasts the thesis of each written work. This embeds two written works as objects of discussion within the dramatic frame.
limits: This does not establish anything about the actual historical existence or content of these works. It only records that the dialogue presents them as objects referenced by characters. The observation does not address the larger frame structure in which this exchange is embedded.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0026

greek_terms:
  - εἴδη
  - αὐτὰ ἄττα
  - μετέχοντα
  - αὐτὴ ὁμοιότης
english_gloss: forms themselves by themselves, participation, likeness itself, form itself by itself
observation: "Parmenides asks Socrates whether he divides forms themselves apart from the things that participate in them, and then immediately lists examples: likeness itself, one and many, and everything heard from Zeno. He then adds justice itself, beautiful itself, good itself, and all such things (130a-b)."
textual_basis: "At 130b, Parmenides asks Socrates whether he divides 'forms themselves apart' from 'the things that participate in them.' He then enumerates form-candidates: likeness itself, one and many, justice itself by itself, beautiful, good, and all such things. This continues the form-object introduction pattern seen in other dialogues."
limits: Parmenides asks rather than asserts; the passage tests whether Socrates holds the separation thesis. The list includes both mathematical-like items (one, many) and evaluative items (justice, beauty, good), but this observation does not adjudicate whether all are intended as forms of the same kind.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 130a-b, after Socrates finishes speaking, Parmenides says Socrates is worthy of admiration for his impulse toward arguments, and immediately follows with a testing question about whether Socrates really divides forms from participants. The praise and critical questioning are juxtaposed.
limits: This observation notes the juxtaposition of praise with critical questioning as a structural pattern. It does not assert that Parmenides' admiration is insincere, only that the passage combines a deference marker with an elenctic move, fitting the mock-deference pattern.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0029
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 131b-131c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 131b-131c
  start_marker: 131b
  end_marker: 131c
  start_char: 9977
  end_char: 10928
  text_sha256: d25ec900d3ccdf5c6fd36232d978664057731ab3171af2b5b80221b7eabe0e86
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Parmenides poses a dilemma for the participation of forms in sensible particulars: if a form is one and the same in many separate things, then either (a) it is present whole in each thing, which would separate it from itself (131b), or (b) only a part of the form is present in each thing, which means the form is divisible and no longer one (131c)."
textual_basis: "At 131b, Parmenides states that if a form is one and the same in many separate things as a whole, it would be separated from itself. Socrates offers the analogy of a single day that is present simultaneously in many places without being separated from itself (131b). Parmenides then introduces the sail (histion) analogy: a sail covering many people is not whole on each person, but a part on each (131c). This yields the conclusion that the forms themselves would be divisible (merista) and participants would partake of a part, not the whole (131c)."
limits: This observation notes the structure of the dilemma at 131b-131c, not whether Parmenides endorses the conclusion in the broader dialogue. It does not claim that the sail analogy is decisive; it only records that the dilemma is posed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - ἡμέρα
  - ἱστίον
english_gloss: day; sail
observation: Parmenides criticizes Socrates' analogy of the day by replacing it with the analogy of a sail (histion) covering many people, showing that Socrates' analogy fails to capture the relation of a form to its participants.
textual_basis: "Socrates proposes at 131b that a form is like one and the same day (hēmera), which is simultaneously present in many places without being separated from itself. Parmenides replies at 131b that Socrates is making what is one and the same be simultaneously in many places, and introduces the sail analogy (histion) at 131b-131c: a sail draped over many people has only a part on each, not the whole. The sail analogy is used to criticize and supersede the day analogy."
limits: This observation records only the replacement of one analogy by another within the argumentative exchange at 131b-131c. It does not assess which analogy is correct or whether Parmenides' criticism succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0031
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 131b-131c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 131b-131c
  start_marker: 131b
  end_marker: 131c
  start_char: 9977
  end_char: 10928
  text_sha256: d25ec900d3ccdf5c6fd36232d978664057731ab3171af2b5b80221b7eabe0e86
greek_terms:
  - μέρος
english_gloss: divisible; part
observation: "Parmenides draws a reductio conclusion from the sail analogy: if the forms are divisible (merista) and participants partake of a part (meros) rather than the whole, then the one form would no longer be one (131c). Socrates immediately rejects that consequence as impossible."
textual_basis: 'At 131c, Parmenides asks: "Are you then willing, Socrates, to say that the one form is in truth divided (merizesthai), and will it still be one?" Socrates answers "not at all" (oudamōs). Parmenides had just stated that the forms themselves would be divisible (merista) and that participants would partake of a part (merous ... metechoi) rather than the whole (131c). The structure is a reductio: the sail analogy implies a divisible form, but Socrates cannot accept a divisible form, so the original participation thesis is in trouble.'
limits: This observation records the reductio structure at 131c. It does not track how the argument is developed after 131c. At 131c only the beginning of the next argumentative move is visible.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - μεταλαμβάνειν
  - μέρος
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Parmenides presses the part-participation branch of the dilemma: if each large thing receives only a part of largeness, it would be large by something smaller than largeness itself; parallel absurdities follow for equality and smallness.
textual_basis: |
  At 131d-131e Parmenides applies the part-branch to largeness, equality, and smallness, then asks whether any participant would choose to have one of the form's parts rather than the whole. The passage closes with the question whether participation is possible by parts or wholes.
limits: |
  This observation records the part-participation absurdities and the final part-or-whole question. It does not repeat the earlier whole-form branch or Socrates's aporia, both of which are covered nearby.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  After Parmenides presents the participation-by-part dilemma at 131d-131e,
  Socrates swears an oath and confesses that the problem seems anything but
  easy to resolve, marking an explicit aporia.
textual_basis: |
  At 131e, Socrates swears by Zeus and says that the matter does not seem easy
  at all to determine. This is a classic Socratic aporia
  expression in response to a refutative dilemma.
limits: |
  This observation notes the aporia as a reported state; it does not claim
  that no solution exists elsewhere in the dialogue or corpus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 132b Socrates proposes that each form might be a thought in souls. After Parmenides tests that proposal at 132c, Socrates rejects the result and at 132d proposes that the forms are paradigms and that participation consists in being made like them.
limits: This observation records the revision sequence from the thought model to the paradigm model. The forced alternative used to test the thought model is recorded separately.
```

```yaml
  The likeness model of participation is rejected and Parmenides directs the inquiry toward a new mode of participation. The argument reaches a point where one candidate account of participation (likeness) is defeated by regress, and the conclusion is that something else must be sought for how things participate in forms.
textual_basis: |
  At 132e-133a, after the regress argument, Parmenides states that other things do not participate in forms through likeness, but something else must be sought by which they participate. Socrates assents with a phrase meaning "it seems so." This revises the account of participation away from the likeness model.
limits: |
  The observation records a revision prompted by objection within a single argument exchange. It does not claim a broader definitional revision across the whole dialogue, nor does it claim that the new mode of participation is found in this span.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: After Socrates reaffirms that particulars get their names by partaking of forms such as likeness, largeness, beauty, and justice (130e-131a), Parmenides asks whether each thing that partakes does so by partaking of the whole form or a part of it, and then asks whether any other mode of participation could occur besides these (131a).
limits: This observation records the dilemma being posed, not how it will be answered or developed in subsequent passages.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0043
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 133d-133e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 133d-133e
  start_marker: 133d
  end_marker: 133e
  start_char: 15195
  end_char: 15954
  text_sha256: 15e2ee7065af9ac74f0e4e9668ee511ff516521ce2e2396bff1d1b4ca4123b05
greek_terms:
  - δεσπότης
  - δοῦλός
  - δεσποτεία
  - δουλεία
english_gloss: master, slave, mastership, slavery
observation: |
  Parmenides argues that the relation between the form of mastership and the form of slavery is a self-referential relation between forms — "mastership itself is of slavery itself what it is" (133e) — and that this relation does not extend to particular humans in the sensible world. A particular human master is master of a particular human slave, not of the form of slavery, and vice versa.
textual_basis: |
  At 133d-e Parmenides states that if someone among us is a master or slave, that person is not a slave of the form Master itself (auto tou despotou, ho esti despotes) nor is the master a master of the form Slave itself (auto doulou, ho esti doulos), but both are human-to-human relations. He then asserts that mastership itself (aute de despoteia) is of slavery itself (autes douleias) what it is, and likewise slavery itself is of mastership itself. The things among us do not have their power (dunamin) toward those forms, nor those toward us, but the forms are themselves of themselves and toward themselves.
limits: |
  The observation records the self-referential relation claim about forms and its separation from sensible particulars. It does not assess whether this argument succeeds, whether it is consistent with earlier participation language, or whether it represents Parmenides' own view or a dialectical move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0044
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 133d-133e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 133d-133e
  start_marker: 133d
  end_marker: 133e
  start_char: 15195
  end_char: 15954
  text_sha256: 15e2ee7065af9ac74f0e4e9668ee511ff516521ce2e2396bff1d1b4ca4123b05
greek_terms:
  - ὁμώνυμα
english_gloss: homonymous, having the same name
observation: |
  At 133d the things among us (ta par' hemin) are described as homonymous (homonuma) with the forms. We are named after them through participation, but the homonymous things among us are themselves toward themselves (auta pros hauta) and not toward the forms (ou pros ta eide).
textual_basis: |
  At 133d Parmenides says the things among us, being homonymous with those forms, are themselves in relation to themselves but not to the forms, and are of themselves but not of those forms in all cases where they are named that way (hosa au onomazetai houtos). This restates and sharpens the earlier participation-as-likeness language (homoiomata) into a homonymy relation that nonetheless denies reciprocal relation between sensibles and forms.
limits: |
  This observation notes the homonymy characterization and the denial of reciprocal relation. It does not adjudicate whether this revises or contradicts the participation model, or whether Parmenides is testing a consequence of the young Socrates' position.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0045
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 133d-133e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 133d-133e
  start_marker: 133d
  end_marker: 133e
  start_char: 15195
  end_char: 15954
  text_sha256: 15e2ee7065af9ac74f0e4e9668ee511ff516521ce2e2396bff1d1b4ca4123b05
greek_terms:
  - ὁμοιώματα
  - μετέχοντες
english_gloss: likenesses, participating
observation: |
  At 133d Parmenides pairs two characterizations of how sensibles relate to forms: they are likenesses (homoiomata) of the forms, or however one wants to put it (eite hopei de tis auta tithetai), and it is through participating (metechontes) in them that we are named as each thing. This juxtaposition treats likeness language and participation language as alternative or compatible descriptions of the same relation, while the subsequent argument goes on to deny any reciprocal power between sensibles and forms.
textual_basis: |
  At 133d the phrase "whether as likenesses or however one posits them, through participating in which we are called each thing" (eite homoiomata eite hopei de tis auta tithetai, hon hemeis metechontes einai hekasta eponomazometha) explicitly pairs the likeness model with participation vocabulary and identifies naming (eponomazometha) as the downstream effect of participation.
limits: |
  This observation records the pairing of likeness and participation language at this juncture. It does not claim that Parmenides endorses either model or that the pairing resolves tensions in the theory of forms. It also does not track how participation language was used earlier in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0046
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 132a-132b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  start_char: 11716
  end_char: 12642
  text_sha256: fba661d3e0f8ba16fbb39fec819977075775bfd55d56b1dbf80fb4994cbd5f1a
greek_terms:
  - ἄπειρα τὸ πλῆθος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Parmenides extends the one-over-many pattern to generate a regress: if the large form and the many large things are taken together as a new plurality, a further form of largeness appears over them, and this repeats, making the forms infinite in number rather than one each."
textual_basis: "Parmenides begins from Socrates's assumption that a single form is grasped from many large things (132a). He then asks: when one looks at the form of largeness together with the many large things, will not another single largeness appear over them all? The assent 'it seems so' (eoiken) is followed by the conclusion that another form will appear, and then another over all those, so that each form will no longer be one but infinite in multiplicity (132a-b)."
limits: This observation records the regress argument structure; it does not evaluate whether the regress is valid or whether Plato endorses it. The limits concern the explicit textual moves at 132a-b only.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Parmenides immediately refines Socrates's proposal by adding that a thought must be a thought of something (tinos), not of nothing, and Socrates assents to both the impossibility of a thought of nothing and the requirement that it be of something.
textual_basis: "At 132b Parmenides asks: 'What then? Is each of the thoughts one, but a thought of nothing?' Socrates replies this is impossible. Parmenides adds: 'But of something?' and Socrates assents. This step-by-step refinement constrains the noema proposal before the next critique."
limits: This records the dialectical refinement step within 132a-b only. It does not cover the subsequent argument that the thought must be of something that is.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0050
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 133b-133c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 133b-133c
  start_marker: 133b
  end_marker: 133c
  start_char: 14286
  end_char: 15195
  text_sha256: a3621be59e40546bfbd0493513ae0f671c6a8072bb36314de1dffcea15e1febd
greek_terms:
  - εἶδος
  - ἕκαστον τῶν ὄντων
  - γιγνώσκεσθαι
  - ἄγνωστα
  - ἐν ἡμῖν
  - ἰδεῶν
  - πρὸς ἀλλήλας
  - πρὸς αὑτὰς
english_gloss: Parmenides raises the difficulty that forms, existing separately by themselves, may be unknowable to us.
observation: Parmenides states that the greatest difficulty in positing a single form for each being is that such forms, if they are separate in themselves, might not be present in us and thus could be unknowable.
textual_basis: At 133b, Parmenides calls this difficulty the greatest in count (megiston de tode). He introduces the hypothetical objector who claims forms cannot be known (133b), then at 133c argues that anyone who posits a separate essence (autēn tina kath' hautēn hekastou ousian) would have to agree that none of these forms is in us (mēdemian autōn einai en hēmin). Socrates concedes the point immediately.
limits: This passage does not resolve whether forms are in fact unknowable; it only reports the difficulty as a hypothetical consequence of positing separate forms. The observation does not claim that Parmenides or Plato endorses the conclusion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ὁμώνυμα
english_gloss: Forms relate to one another according to their own being, not to things in our world.
observation: At 133c-133d, Parmenides asserts that the Forms are what they are in relation to each other and have their being in relation to themselves, not in relation to things in our realm that share their names.
textual_basis: "The 133c-133d clause completes the contrast: Forms have their being in relation to themselves and not in relation to the things among us, while the things among us are homonymous with them and relate to their own kind."
limits: This records the mutual Form-relation contrast and the homonymy contrast with things among us. It does not assert that this is Plato's final view or resolve the unknowability objection that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0052
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 133b-133c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 133b-133c
  start_marker: 133b
  end_marker: 133c
  start_char: 14286
  end_char: 15195
  text_sha256: a3621be59e40546bfbd0493513ae0f671c6a8072bb36314de1dffcea15e1febd
greek_terms:
  - μὴ ἀφυής
  - πόρρωθεν πραγματευομένου
  - ἕπεσθαι
  - ἀπίθανος
english_gloss: Defending the forms against the unknowability objection requires an experienced, capable respondent willing to follow a long and remote argument; otherwise the defender remains unconvincing.
observation: "At 133b-133c, Parmenides describes the demanding conditions for a successful defense of the knowability of forms: the objector must be experienced in many things (pollōn empeiros), not without natural ability (mē aphuēs), and willing to follow a defender who works through many things from afar (porrōthen pragmateuomenou), or else the defender will be unconvincing (apithanos) in forcing the conclusion that forms are unknowable."
textual_basis: Parmenides states that you could not demonstrate the objector's error unless the disputant happened to be experienced in many things and not untalented, and were willing to follow the demonstrator proceeding from far off through much material (133b-133c). The passage ends with the remark that otherwise the one compelling the conclusion that forms are unknowable would be unconvincing (apithanos).
limits: This observation records the procedural requirements stated for defending forms. It does not claim that such a defense is possible or will be supplied in the dialogue. It does not assess whether the young Socrates meets these requirements.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0054

  - αἱ ἰδέαι
  - γένος τι ἑκάστου
  - οὐσία αὐτὴ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides refers to the forms using multiple designations — eide, ideai of beings, each form itself, a certain genos of each thing, and ousia itself — while insisting that these difficulties and many others necessarily attend the positing of forms.
textual_basis: At 135a, Parmenides says that the forms (ta eide) necessarily have these and many other difficulties if these ideai of beings exist and one marks off each form as something itself (auto ti hekaston eidos). He then adds that a man of very good natural gifts is needed to learn that there is a certain genos of each thing and an ousia itself.
limits: This observation notes the variegated naming of forms. It does not assess whether the multiplicity of terms signals distinct subspecies of form or mere stylistic variation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0055
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 134e-135a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  text_sha256: 170c9868449769758a73a43bcefa51ad056a87b55a9246ac8477d2a993c2eef8
greek_terms:
  - ἀπορεῖν
  - ἀμφισβητεῖν
  - ἄγνωστα
  - δυσανάπειστον
  - εὐφυοῦς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides warns that the difficulties attending the forms cause the hearer to be at a loss (aporein) and to dispute that these things exist, and that even if they most certainly exist, they must necessarily be unknowable to human nature — and that saying this seems to say something and is astonishingly hard to convince someone of.
textual_basis: "At 135a, Parmenides says: 'so that the hearer is at a loss and disputes that these things exist, and that even if they most certainly exist, it is a great necessity that they be unknowable to human nature (tei anthropinei physei agnosta), and saying these things both seems to say something and, as we just said, is astonishingly hard to persuade (dysanapeiston) someone of.' He immediately adds that a man of very good natural gifts (euphyous) is needed to learn about genos and ousia itself."
limits: This records the reported aporia effect and the claim about unknowability. It does not assert that Plato endorses the unknowability thesis or that the aporia is the dialogue's final word.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0056
source_work: Parmenides

greek_terms:
  - εἴδη τῶν ὄντων
  - ἰδέαν τῶν ὄντων ἑκάστου τὴν αὐτὴν ἀεὶ
  - τὴν τοῦ διαλέγεσθαι δύναμιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Parmenides argues that rejecting Forms makes thought and dialectic impossible: without marking off a Form for each thing, one's thought has nowhere to turn and the power of dialectic is utterly destroyed."
textual_basis: At 135b-c Parmenides states that if someone, having looked at all the difficulties just raised, will not allow Forms of beings and will not mark off a Form for each individual thing, he will have nowhere to turn his thought, since he will not allow an Idea of each being that is always the same, and thus he will utterly destroy the power of dialectic.
limits: This observation records Parmenides' explicit claim within the dramatic frame. It does not establish that the argument is valid, that Plato endorses it without qualification, or that the earlier difficulties have actually been resolved.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: |
  Zeno states that without a thoroughgoing passage and wandering through all things it is impossible to encounter the truth and attain understanding. This methodological claim about the necessity of exhaustive dialectical traversal is made before Parmenides begins his exercise.
textual_basis: |
  Zeno says at 136e that the many do not know that without this thoroughgoing passage and wandering through all things it is impossible to encounter the truth and attain understanding. The key expression is quoted by Zeno as the necessary condition for reaching truth.
limits: |
  The remark does not specify the method's steps or guarantee success; it only asserts the necessity of comprehensive dialectical practice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0059
source_work: Parmenides

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

greek_terms:
  - δεῖ γὰρ χαρίζεσθαι
english_gloss: must obey, must gratify
observation: |
  After Zeno's methodological claim, Pythodorus (reported by Antiphon) says everyone begged Parmenides to demonstrate what he meant and not do otherwise. Parmenides replies that it is necessary to obey, and that he must gratify the company since they are alone together. The passage records a procedural agreement that compels Parmenides to undertake the exercise.
textual_basis: |
  At 136e the company, including Aristoteles (the youngest), begs Parmenides to demonstrate what he means. Parmenides answers that it is necessary to obey, and at 137a adds that he must gratify since, as Zeno notes, they are by themselves. The combination of necessity and the private audience sets up the procedural commitment.
limits: |
  The observation records the dramatic mechanism by which the demonstration is secured. It does not analyze whether Parmenides actually wants to undertake the exercise or assess the quality of the arguments that follow.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Parmenides praises Socrates at 135d-e for refusing to investigate confusion in visible things and instead directing inquiry toward those things that one can most grasp by reason and would consider to be forms (eide).
textual_basis: At 135e Parmenides states that Socrates did not permit confusion to be examined in visible things (ta horomena) nor concerning them, but rather concerning those things one would most grasp by reason (logo) and would consider to be forms (eide).
limits: This is a methodological endorsement of the forms-versus-sensibles contrast, not a proof that forms exist. The observation records the contrast as stated by Parmenides, not as an independent metaphysical claim.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0063
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 134a-134b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 134a-134b
  start_marker: 134a
  end_marker: 134b
  start_char: 15954
  end_char: 16854
  text_sha256: 47206422799337d5da923f08e0f9d6b72db01f81c05f6698b70b6dfd1a4dad20
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides argues that the Form of Knowledge itself knows the Forms themselves, and each particular branch of knowledge knows each particular being. He then infers that human knowledge can only be of things among us, not of the Forms, because we do not possess the Form of Knowledge itself and cannot partake of it (134a-134b).
textual_basis: At 134a Parmenides establishes a parallel between the Forms in themselves and the things among us, then poses that episteme itself knows truth itself. He distinguishes the Form of Knowledge from the particular knowledges we have, and at 134b concludes that since we do not possess the Forms (as previously agreed) and do not partake of the Form of Knowledge itself, the Forms are unknowable to us.
limits: This observation records the argumentative move of attributing cognitive properties to the Forms themselves and deriving an unknowability consequence. It does not assess whether this conclusion is Parmenides' final position or is later qualified elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0064
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 134a-134b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 134a-134b
  start_marker: 134a
  end_marker: 134b
  start_char: 15954
  end_char: 16854
  text_sha256: 47206422799337d5da923f08e0f9d6b72db01f81c05f6698b70b6dfd1a4dad20
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides makes the unknowability of the Forms by us depend explicitly on our failure to partake of the Form of Knowledge itself (134b). The conclusion that we do not know any Form follows from the premise that we do not partake of the Form of Knowledge itself.
textual_basis: At 134b Parmenides recapitulates that we neither possess the Forms nor can they be among us, then states that the Forms are known by the Form of Knowledge itself. The explicit inference makes non-participation in the Form of Knowledge itself the causal ground for the unknowability result.
limits: The observation records the explicit causal link between non-participation and unknowability. It does not evaluate whether the argument's premise that we lack the Forms entails that we cannot participate in the Form of Knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The segment 134a-134b contains a dense sequence of Socrates' assents building to the conclusion that the Forms are unknowable. After Parmenides establishes the parallel structure between Forms and things among us (134a), Socrates assents repeatedly, leading to the final inference at 134b that the Forms are unknown to us.
textual_basis: "The passage moves through several short question-assent pairs: the distinction between knowledge itself and particular knowledges (134a), the restriction of human knowledge to things among us (134a-b), the reminder that we do not possess the Forms (134b), and the claim that Forms are known by the Form of Knowledge itself (134b). Each assent builds toward the conclusion."
limits: This records the assent-chain structure of the argument. It does not analyze whether the assents commit Socrates to a position he or the dialogue later retracts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ὡς ὄντος
  - ὡς οὐκ ὄντος
  - γυμνασάμενος
  - κυρίως διόψεσθαι τὸ ἀληθές
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Parmenides declares the gymnastic method: posit a hypothesis and examine what follows both if the subject is and if it is not, for the subject itself and for the others, in order to see the truth authoritatively."
textual_basis: At 136b-136c Parmenides summarizes the method as taking a hypothesis as being and as not being, then examining consequences for the hypothesized subject and for the others. He concludes that only someone trained in this way will see the truth authoritatively.
limits: This records the programmatic method summary. It does not include Socrates' follow-up request for a demonstration or Parmenides' age-based reluctance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates responds to Parmenides' description of the method by calling the exercise an impossible business and says he does not quite understand it, then requests that Parmenides demonstrate by going through an example himself.
textual_basis: At 136c Socrates says that Parmenides describes an impossible business and that he does not quite understand. He immediately asks Parmenides to go through it himself with a hypothesis so he may learn better.
limits: This is an aporia reported by the respondent, not a conclusion of an elenctic refutation; Socrates has not yet attempted the method.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0068

greek_terms:
  - πολὺ ἔργον
  - τηλικῷδε
  - γελάσαντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Both Parmenides and Zeno resist Socrates' request for a demonstration on grounds of the speaker's advanced age: Parmenides calls it a great labor for someone of his age, and Zeno, laughing, supports the refusal by noting how great a task Socrates is imposing and adds that such displays would be unseemly before a large audience especially for someone of that age."
textual_basis: At 136d Parmenides says Socrates commands a great labor for one of such age. When Socrates turns to Zeno, Zeno laughs and says the task is not trivial, remarks that asking before a large audience would be unseemly, and attributes the constraint to Parmenides' age.
limits: The observation records the age-based constraint on speech performance, not a substantive argument about the method.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: 0028b3d03d81bbf030747a162320b792dde825038db4923755b57f803d0ea45b
observation: Parmenides defines round as that whose extremes are everywhere equidistant from the middle, and straight as that whose middle stands before both extremes, at 137e. Aristoteles assents to each definition. These geometric definitions are introduced as a criterion for testing whether the One participates in shape.
textual_basis: At 137e, Parmenides gives two geometric definitions — one for round and one for straight — and Aristoteles assents to both before the conclusion that the One cannot partake of either shape because partaking would require having parts.
limits: These definitions apply only to geometric shape in this argument; they are not claimed as universal definitions of roundness and straightness outside this context.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The sequence rules out being in another at 138a, then continues through the being-in-itself branch at 138a-138b. Each step receives a brief assent, ending in the conclusion that the One is nowhere.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0075
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 138c-138d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 138c-138d
  start_marker: 138c
  end_marker: 138d
  start_char: 25374
  end_char: 26228
  text_sha256: 4a1baa76b974cdf2f846a48e56a9bbe42f3afee1d1c15b759d7a44a2c782c66e
greek_terms:
  - μέσου
  - μέρη
  - ἀδύνατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Parmenides presents the motion of the one as an exhaustive disjunction: if the one moves, it must do so either by circular rotation in the same place or by changing from one place to another (138c). The respondent Aristotle acknowledges this as a necessity.
textual_basis: |
  At 138c, after denying alteration, the argument turns to locomotion with a structured alternative: "if the one is carried, it would either revolve in a circle in the same place or change from one place to another" (eithe…peripher or metallattein choran). Aristotle responds "necessarily" (ananke).
limits: |
  This observation records the forced-disjunction structure only. It does not assess whether the dichotomy is exhaustive in fact or whether the subsequent refutations of both branches are valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0076
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 138c-138d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The circular-motion branch of the locomotion alternative is rejected: circular rotation would require resting on a middle and having parts around the middle, which the One cannot have.
textual_basis: |
  At 138c-138d, Parmenides argues that if the One revolved in the same place it would need a middle on which it rests and other parts carried around that middle. Because the One has no middle or parts, the respondent accepts that this motion is impossible.
limits: |
  This records the reductio of circular motion only. The separate place-change branch is repaired as its own observation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - γίγνεσθαι
  - ἀκίνητον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The place-change branch of locomotion is rejected: coming to be in another place would require the One to be neither wholly outside nor already inside the place into which it comes, and the argument concludes that the One is unmoved.
textual_basis: |
  At 138d-139a Parmenides distinguishes being in something from coming to be in it, then argues that the One cannot be in the transitional condition required for place-change. The passage closes by denying motion and calling the One unmoved.
limits: |
  This records the reductio of place-change and the local immobility conclusion. It does not repeat the circular-motion branch, which is recorded separately.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0077
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 138c-138d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 138c-138d
  start_marker: 138c
  end_marker: 138d
  start_char: 25374
  end_char: 26228
  text_sha256: 4a1baa76b974cdf2f846a48e56a9bbe42f3afee1d1c15b759d7a44a2c782c66e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The respondent Aristotle offers only minimal assents throughout 138c-d: "yes," "impossible," "it doesn't appear so," "necessarily," "none," "if indeed it does," "yes," and an admission of not understanding ("I don't see how") followed by a compelled "necessarily." The interlocutor is reduced to following a chain of forced alternatives without introducing independent content.
textual_basis: |
  From 138c ("nai…adunaton…ou phainetai…isos…ananke") through 138d ("oudemia…eiper ge de…nai…ouk ennoo hope…ananke"), Aristotle's responses are brief assent particles or expressions of inability to see an alternative. The longest response is the admission at 138d that he does not understand how coming to be could be more impossible than being.
limits: |
  This record notes the turn pattern only. It does not claim that Aristotle is depicted as overwhelmed or that Parmenides' reasoning is coercive. The compressed answer pattern is a structural feature of the passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0078
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 136a-136b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides delivers a long methodological instruction spanning multiple hypotheses and topics (one/many, likeness/unlikeness, motion/rest, generation/destruction, being/not-being) in a single continuous speech. No short-answer exchange occurs in this segment; the respondent's only contribution is a brief procedural question at the opening, after which Parmenides delivers an extended exposition.
textual_basis: At 136a the respondent asks how Parmenides means (a short procedural question), and Parmenides responds with a lengthy explanation running through 136b without further interruption. The turn pattern shows the junior interlocutor reduced to a single procedural query while the senior figure controls the entire instructional block.
limits: This observation does not claim the respondent is refuted or that the method itself is defective; it only notes the turn asymmetry and the collapse of dialogue into monologue within this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0079
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 136a-136b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 136a-136b
  start_marker: 136a
  end_marker: 136b
  start_char: 20327
  end_char: 21212
  text_sha256: f91ad5c854247630ecced42df08d070213fa4f1a97921ccfafdb2d1e7122558e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Parmenides prescribes a systematic investigation that must examine consequences both for the hypothesized entity and for other things, both in relation to themselves and in relation to each other, under both the positive hypothesis (that X is) and the negative hypothesis (that X is not). He enumerates a series of paired contraries to test: many/one, likeness/unlikeness, motion/rest, generation/destruction, and being/not-being itself."
textual_basis: "At 136a-b Parmenides lays out the procedure: for any hypothesis, one must investigate what follows for the hypothesized things themselves and for the one, both in relation to themselves and to each other, under both the positive (that it is) and negative (that it is not) versions. He explicitly extends this to likeness, unlikeness, motion, rest, generation, destruction, and being and not-being."
limits: This observation records the method's formal structure as prescribed at 136a-b but does not evaluate whether the dialogue's later application follows this procedure exactly or whether the grid is philosophically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides frames the new methodological exercise by explicit backward reference to Zeno's earlier hypothesis (that the many exist), now repurposed as the first training example. The method is introduced as a training exercise rather than as a direct continuation of the prior dialectical argument.
textual_basis: At 136a Parmenides refers back to the hypothesis which Zeno hypothesized, concerning whether the many are. He also introduces the exercise with the stated purpose of training, explicitly saying the purpose is to be trained.
limits: This observation identifies the framing move within the span but does not assess whether the training status of what follows undermines or strengthens its philosophical force. It also does not claim that Zeno's hypothesis is identical in content to the new investigation, only that it is invoked as a reference point.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: At 138e-139a Parmenides concludes that the One is immobile with respect to every kind of motion, because it has no parts and cannot be in anything, so it can neither change place, rotate in place, nor be altered.
textual_basis: "The argument proceeds by eliminating each motion type: change of place, rotation in the same spot, and alteration, at 139a. The conclusion is stated as an inferential claim that according to every kind of motion the One is immobile (139a)."
limits: This conclusion follows from the prior premises that the One has no parts and cannot be in anything; it does not independently establish immobility. The conclusion applies only within the first hypothesis about the One.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0083
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 138e-139a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 138e-139a
  start_marker: 138e
  end_marker: 139a
  start_char: 26228
  end_char: 27038
  text_sha256: c715b965f157933dd46ee3bde2abdebcffad84aede1c231d374497821d6e4363
greek_terms:
  - μέρη
  - ὅλον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 138e Parmenides builds an assent chain from the One's partlessness to the impossibility of its being in anything, using a two-step argument: first, what has no parts cannot be in something either partly inside and partly outside; second, what has neither parts nor is a whole is even more unable to come to be in something."
textual_basis: Parmenides secures an affirmative assent at 138e for the claim about partless things and their inability to be partly inside and partly outside something, then secures a further affirmative assent for the stronger claim about what lacks both parts and wholeness. These assents feed directly into the 139a conclusions about motion and location.
limits: The assent chain depends on a spatial model of being-in that may not exhaust all senses of presence. The conclusion is bound to the dialectical context of the first hypothesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0084
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 140a-140b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 140a-140b
  start_marker: 140a
  end_marker: 140b
  start_char: 28674
  end_char: 29501
  text_sha256: 36c8eeaf552a79d8dd13236d7f1f05fdce7f2b1f173dc46a6fe7d982ea45ced4
greek_terms:
  - ταὐτὸν
  - ὅμοιον
  - ἕτερον
  - ἀνόμοιον
english_gloss: same, like, other/different, unlike
observation: |
  At 140a-140b, the argument derives that the one is neither like nor unlike either another or itself. The reasoning chains through the predicates 'same-affected' (tauton peponthos) and 'other-affected' (heteron peponthos): if the one were same-affected, it would be like; if other-affected, unlike. Each is rejected because it would require the one to be affected in more ways than being one, which contradicts the hypothesis.
textual_basis: |
  At 140a, the text states that if the one has been affected apart from being one, it would be more than one, which is impossible. From this it excludes same-affectedness and other-affectedness, then derives the exclusion of likeness at 140a-140b ("neither like nor unlike another or itself"). An additional turn at 140b extends the same structure to equality and inequality.
limits: |
  This observation records the exclusion of paired predicates by the hypothesis. It does not assess whether the inference from 'affected in more ways than being one' to excluding likeness/unlikeness is valid, nor does it address the earlier/recurring uses of this exclusion pattern in the hypotheses.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0085

  text_sha256: 36c8eeaf552a79d8dd13236d7f1f05fdce7f2b1f173dc46a6fe7d982ea45ced4
greek_terms:
  - ἴσον
  - ἄνισον
  - μέτρων
english_gloss: equal, unequal, measures
observation: |
  At 140b, after excluding likeness and unlikeness, Parmenides extends the negation to equality and inequality: the one, being such as has been shown, will be neither equal nor unequal either to itself or to another. The move is introduced with 'and indeed' (kai men), marking a further consequence of the same hypothethical framework, and equality is defined as being of the same measures (ton auton metron) as that to which it is equal.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 140b reads: "and indeed, being such, it will be neither equal nor unequal either to itself or to another." This is followed by the question "how?" and the definitional move: "being equal, it will be of the same measures as that to which it is equal." The negation of equality and inequality is presented as a direct consequence of the prior exclusion of likeness and unlikeness.
limits: |
  This observation records the extension of the negation pattern to a new domain (equality/inequality) and the definitional move that accompanies it. It does not assess whether the inference from likeness-exclusion to equality-exclusion is valid, nor does it address the full development of the equality/inequality argument, which begins in this segment but continues beyond 140b.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0086
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 139d-139e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 139d-139e
  start_marker: 139d
  end_marker: 139e
  start_char: 27862
  end_char: 28674
  text_sha256: 1de88fac5e14612bce0b1b8c0033c3fe1f0a53df11a2e8c6136b4fccc6e91867
greek_terms:
  - ταὐτόν
  - ἕτερον
  - ὅμοιον
  - ἀνόμοιον
english_gloss: same, other/different, like, unlike
observation: |
  At 139d-139e, the argument deduces that the one can be neither same nor different, neither like nor unlike — whether in relation to itself or to another. The deduction proceeds through the identity of the nature of the one with the nature of the same (139d), leading to the conclusion that sameness would make the one not-one, which is impossible. The exclusion of likeness and unlikeness is then derived from the prior exclusion of sameness and difference (139e).
textual_basis: |
  From 139d: "if the one and the same do not differ at all, whenever something became the same it would always become one, and whenever one, same" (139d-139e). The conclusion that sameness is impossible for the one leads to the exclusion of both otherness and sameness (139e). Likeness and unlikeness are then excluded because likeness requires having the same attribute, already separated from the nature of the one (139e).
limits: |
  The observation records the structural deduction within the second hypothesis (if the one is). It does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether Parmenides endorses it, or whether the conclusion applies to Platonic forms outside this dialectical exercise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0087
source_work: Parmenides

source_ref:

  text_sha256: 1de88fac5e14612bce0b1b8c0033c3fe1f0a53df11a2e8c6136b4fccc6e91867
greek_terms:
  - ταὐτόν
  - ἕν
  - πολλὰ
  - ἕτερον
  - ὅμοιον
english_gloss: same, one, many, different, like
observation: |
  At 139d-139e, the argument reaches the conclusion that the one cannot be same or different, like or unlike, through a chain of reductio steps. If the one were the same as itself, it would become not-one, which is impossible (139d-139e). The impossibility is marked with "but this is impossible" (139e), and the conclusion is stated as a double exclusion: the one is neither different nor the same, neither to itself nor to another.
textual_basis: |
  The argument at 139d explicitly derives an impossibility: "if then the one will be the same as itself, it will not be one with itself; and thus being one it will not be one. But this is impossible" (139d-139e). The conclusion is then stated: "thus the one is neither different nor the same, either to itself or to another" (139e).
limits: |
  This records the reductio structure within the dialectical exercise of the second hypothesis. It does not assert that the conclusion represents Parmenides' or Plato's own view, nor does it evaluate the validity of the identification of the nature of the one with the nature of the same.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ὅμοιον
  - πεπονθὸς
  - χωρὶς
english_gloss: same, like, having-suffered, separate
observation: |
  At 139e, the argument establishes a definitional connection between sameness and likeness through an assent-based move: "that which has suffered the same is like" (139e). Aristotle (the respondent) assents ("yes"). This definition then grounds the subsequent exclusion: since the nature of the same was shown to be separate from the one, the one cannot be like anything.
textual_basis: |
  At 139e: "that which has somehow suffered the same is like" — Aristotle assents. The text then states: "the same appeared separate in nature from the one" (139e). The conjunction of these two steps — a definitional equation and the prior result — yields the conclusion that the one is neither like nor unlike.
limits: |
  The observation records the definitional step within the dialectical deduction. It does not claim that this definition of likeness is Plato's considered view, nor does it address the metaphysics of the same/like relation outside the context of the Parmenides deductions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Parmenides characterizes the ensuing dialectical exercise as laborious play (pragmateiode paidian), framing the serious logical investigation that follows under the sign of a game.
textual_basis: At 137b, before launching the hypotheses about the one, Parmenides says since it seems good to play a laborious game (epeideper dokei pragmateiode paidian paizein), explicitly labeling the method as play even though it involves rigorous argumentation.
limits: This does not prove that Parmenides considers the content unserious; the adjective pragmateiodes (laborious, systematic) modifies the play metaphor, suggesting a serious game rather than mere frivolity. The observation records only the self-characterization, not an interpretive claim about the dialogue's ultimate purpose.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 137b-c, Parmenides asks who will answer and immediately proposes the youngest (ho neotatos), offering two explicit reasons: the youngest is least likely to be meddlesome or over-elaborate (hekista gar an polypragmonoi) and will answer according to what he actually thinks (ha oietai). Aristoteles identifies himself as the youngest and accepts the role at 137c."
limits: This observation records only the explicit selection criteria and the named respondent. It does not claim that this selection pattern recurs across dialogues, nor does it interpret the dramatic significance of the choice beyond what is stated.
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - εἰ ἕν ἐστιν
english_gloss: if one is, many
observation: "Parmenides opens the dialectical exercise by positing a formal hypothesis (if one is), and immediately derives a consequence: that the one would not be many. The first deduction then unfolds: if the one is not many, it can have neither parts nor be a whole."
textual_basis: "At 137c, Parmenides states the hypothesis explicitly (ei hen estin), secures assent to the impossibility of the one being many (pos gar an;), and then moves through a chain: no parts → no whole, because a part is part of a whole and a whole is that from which no part is missing. This establishes the dialectical starting point for the entire set of deductions that follow."
limits: This observation records only the formal structure of the hypothesis and its first deduction. It does not assess whether the reasoning is valid, nor does it claim that this method of hypothesis is unique to the Parmenides. It is limited to the explicit logical steps in 137c.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0092

greek_terms:
  - ἦν
  - γέγονε
  - ἐγίγνετο
  - ποτὲ γεγονότος
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 141d the argument extends the temporal exclusion already established for the one: because the one does not participate in any temporal affection such as becoming older or younger simultaneously, it does not partake of time at all and is not in any time. The vocabulary of past-tense markers is then explicitly identified as signifying participation in a past time.
textual_basis: |
  The passage moves from denying the one's participation in specific temporal pathēmata (older/younger becoming, established earlier in 141d) to the general conclusion at 141d: the one partakes in no time and is not in any time. Immediately after, Parmenides asks whether the past-tense expressions — to ēn, to gegone, to egigneto — signify participation in a past time, and the answer is affirmative.
limits: |
  This records the logical extension of temporal non-participation to a general exclusion of the one from time, and the explicit semantic analysis of past-tense vocabulary as time-participation markers. It does not address whether the same analysis is applied to future-tense markers later in the passage, nor does it evaluate the Parmenidean thesis itself.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: At 140e-141a, Parmenides argues that the One cannot be older, younger, or of the same age as either itself or another, because age-equality and age-inequality depend on participation in likeness/unlikeness and equality/inequality, from which the One was previously excluded.
textual_basis: 'The argument at 140e-141a recapitulates prior deductions: having the same age would require participation in equality of time and similarity ("isoteitos chronou kai homoioteitos"), but the One was earlier said not to partake of likeness or equality; likewise it was said not to partake of unlikeness or inequality. Since temporal-age predicates require these participations, the One cannot bear any age predicate relative to itself or another.'
limits: This observation records the excluded-property argument structure. It does not assess whether the earlier denials of participation were valid, nor does it resolve the resulting paradox that the One appears outside time.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0102
source_work: Parmenides

greek_terms:
  - ταὐτόν
  - ἕτερον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The one is denied the possibility of being either the same (tauton) as another or different (heteron) from itself, and likewise denied being different from another or different from itself, through an argument that relates these predicates to what being "one" permits (139b-139c).
textual_basis: |
  At 139b the argument states that if the one were different from itself it would be other than one and would not be one. If it were the same as another, it would be that other and not itself as one (139b-c). The conclusion inverts the frame: the one will not be different from another while it is one, because it is proper only to what is different (hetero(i)) to be different from another, not to the one (139c). The one is therefore neither same as another nor different from anything.
limits: |
  This observation records the relational-opposition structure of the argument from 139b-139c. It does not assess the validity of the reasoning or its place in the broader first hypothesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0103
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 139b-139c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

greek_terms:
  - προσήκει
  - μόνῳ ἑτέρῳ ἑτέρου
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The argument at 139c delimits the proper scope of the predicate "different" (heteron): it belongs only to what is different (hetero(i)) to be different from another, and to nothing else, so that the one, insofar as it is one, cannot bear this predicate.
textual_basis: |
  At 139c Aristotle (the respondent) assents to the claim that it is not proper (prosekei) to the one to be different from something, but only to what is different (mono(i) hetero(i)) to be different from another, and to nothing else. This restricts what a predicate can properly apply to and uses that restriction to deny the predicate to the one.
limits: |
  This observation records the scope-delimitation move within the argument. It does not assess whether the restriction is independently justified or generalizable to other predicates.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - μετέχον
  - τοῦ αὐτοῦ
  - μέτρων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The argument denies equality and inequality relations to the One through repeated participation (metechein) vocabulary applied to "the same" (to auto) and "measures" (metra). The One is said not to partake in sameness of measure, in one measure, in many measures, in few measures, or in the same at all.
textual_basis: "At 140c Parmenides asserts it is impossible for something not partaking in the same (to me metechon tou autou) to be of the same measures, denying equality to the One. At 140d the conclusion stacks negations by mode of non-participation: the One does not partake of one measure (henos metrou metechon), nor of many, nor of few, nor of the same at all (to parapan tou autou metechon)."
limits: This observation registers the use of participation vocabulary in a dialectical deduction about quantitative relations; it does not claim that "the same" here refers to a Form or that the argument depends on a theory of Forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The deduction blocks quantitative predication of the One by deriving a part-whole consequence: if the One has multiple measures, it has as many parts as measures, and therefore would no longer be one but many."
textual_basis: At 140c-d Parmenides argues that if the One has more measures or fewer, it would have as many parts as measures (tosouton kai meron an eie), and thus it would no longer be one but as many as the measures (ouketi hen estai alla tosauta osaper kai ta metra).
limits: This observation identifies a specific deductive move within the second hypothesis of the Parmenides; it does not claim that this argument exhausts or represents the whole of the dialogue's treatment of part-whole relations.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0106

  text_sha256: 73f3b85692bfbe0226d1c504dcf1c1933656a3c56587bd15bcc220bde1829a0b
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides leads the respondent Aristoteles through a sequence of short assents from the hypothesis 'if the one is' to the necessary existence of number, an unlimited multitude of beings, and the distribution of being over all things.
textual_basis: "At 144a, the chain moves from odd-times-odd, even-times-odd, and odd-times-even (all assented to) to the claim that no number is left out, and from 'if the one is, number must exist' to 'if number exists, there would be many things and an unlimited multitude of beings.' At 144b the chain continues: if every number participates in being, then each part of number participates in it, and being is distributed over all beings."
limits: This observation records the assent-chain structure, not the soundness of the deduction or the metaphysical status of the claims about being.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - μεμέρισται πάντων μάλιστα
  - οὐσία
  - μετέχων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Within the deductive argument from the hypothesis 'if the one is,' Parmenides deploys participation vocabulary to claim that being is distributed over all many beings, never absent from any being, and is fragmented and divided most of all into beings of every size.
textual_basis: At 144a, number is said to participate in ousia. At 144b, ousia is said to be distributed over all many beings and to be absent from none; it is then described as fragmented into the smallest, greatest, and beings in every way, and divided most of all.
limits: This observation records the use of participation and distribution vocabulary for being within a deductive context. It does not assess whether the fragmentation of being is compatible with the unity of the form or with other participation claims made elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: "The argument derives a sequence of number properties from units and pairs: each member of a pair is one, one added to a pair yields three, and the resulting two/three structure generates odd, even, twice, thrice, and even-times-even terms."
textual_basis: At 143d, each pair-member is counted as one and one joined to any pair gives three. At 143d-e, three is marked as odd, two as even, and the existence of two and three is used to generate twice, thrice, and the paired multiplicative formulations.
limits: This observation records the local number-generation sequence in 143d-143e. It does not claim that the passage derives every possible number or proves number independently of the hypothesis under examination.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0110
source_work: Parmenides
```
