```yaml
  text_sha256: 817a912e583a8772c065fa04a2a30a402ba0f061e78ef42a34fb5627e0404b58
greek_terms:
  - ἔστι
  - γίγνεται
  - ἔσται
  - γενήσεται
  - γενηθήσεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The argument in 141e exhaustively denies the One every temporal mode: past, present, and future. It lists the past verbal forms (gegonenai, egigneto, ēn) and denies them; lists present forms (gegonen, gignetai, estin) and denies them; lists future forms (genēsetai, genēthēsetai, estai) and denies them. The denial follows from the prior conclusion that the One in no way partakes of time."
textual_basis: The passage enumerates three temporal frames (past, present, future) and denies the One every verbal form within each, concluding that the One partakes of being in none of these ways, hence the One is not.
limits: This observation records the systematic exclusion of temporal modes; it does not assess whether the argument is valid or whether the conclusion contradicts earlier hypotheses in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0111

  - ἀληθέστατα
  - οὐκ ἔστιν
  - οὐκ ἔοικεν
  - οὐ φαίνεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 141e the interlocutor delivers a series of short affirmative assents that lock in each deductive step. The respondent agrees that future-tense expressions belong to the future, that present-tense expressions belong to the present, that the conditional consequence follows from non-participation in time, that being cannot be participated in otherwise than through temporal modes, and finally that the One in no way is. The passage culminates in the conclusion that the One is not.
textual_basis: "The exchange proceeds through paired question-and-assent units: the mapping of temporal expressions to time divisions, the conditional inference from non-participation in time, the question about participation in being, and the final negation of the One's existence."
limits: This records the assent-chain structure; it does not evaluate whether the respondent should have assented at each step or whether the argument hiddenly shifts the meaning of being.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0112

english_gloss: ""
observation: At 141e the argument makes the One's participation in being depend entirely on participation in time. The text asks whether anything could partake of being otherwise than according to one of the temporal modes (past, present, future) and answers that it cannot. Since the One partakes of time in no way, it consequently partakes of being in no way.
textual_basis: The explicit bridging question at 141e is whether anything could share in being otherwise than according to past, present, or future modes, and the respondent answers that it cannot. This equates modes of being with temporal modes.
limits: This records the argument's dependence of being-participation on time-participation; it does not assess whether this dependence is Platonic doctrine or a dialectical move specific to the first hypothesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - πεπερασμένον
  - ἀρχὴν
  - μέσον
  - τελευτήν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The 144e-145a deduction gives the one-being paired formal predicates: it is many and unlimited in multitude because it is fragmented by being, and it is limited with beginning, middle, and end because its parts are contained by a whole."
textual_basis: At 144e the One is said to be fragmented by being and therefore many and unlimited in multitude. At 144e-145a the whole is then treated as what contains the parts, so the One is limited by the whole and, as a whole, has beginning, middle, and end.
limits: This observation records the local catalogue of formal predicates generated in this step. It does not claim these predicates are enduring features of Platonic Forms outside this hypothesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0114
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 142e-143a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 142e-143a
  start_marker: 142e
  end_marker: 143a
  start_char: 34864
  end_char: 35686
  text_sha256: d72730fa9253db76f0dc679f19c5773dbe73b57562fcf515ef50a15c12d4204e
greek_terms:
  - μόριον
  - ἄπειρον
  - πλῆθος
english_gloss: the one-being, part, unlimited, multitude
observation: "The argument generates an infinite regress: the one-being has two parts, 'one' and 'being'; each part itself is a one-being and thus splits into two further parts, so that the one-being necessarily becomes 'two always coming to be and never one,' and therefore 'unlimited in multitude.'"
textual_basis: At 142e-143a, Parmenides argues that since the one-being contains one and being as parts, and each of these parts in turn 'holds fast to both the one and being,' the smallest part is always composed of two parts; the process continues ad infinitum (kata ton auton logon houtos aei), yielding the conclusion that the one-being is unlimited in multitude.
limits: The observation records the structure of the regress argument and its conclusion; it does not assess whether the regress is vicious or benign, nor does it evaluate the argument's validity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0115
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 144c-144d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 144c-144d
  start_marker: 144c
  end_marker: 144d
  start_char: 38160
  end_char: 39018
  text_sha256: a36b23e54b0acf1995640095e9889891b5a3b7e692f86e91b12edaec021aadd5
greek_terms:
  - μέρη ἀπέραντα τῆς οὐσίας
  - πλεῖστα μέρη
  - μεμερισμένον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Parmenides argues that if the One is, it participates in ousia, and ousia has infinitely many parts (144c). He then shows that the One must be present to each part of ousia, and since it cannot be present as a whole simultaneously in many places, it must be divided (144c-144d). From this he derives a consequence that ousia has been distributed into not more parts than the One.
textual_basis: |
  The passage moves from "the parts of ousia are unlimited" (144c) through the claim that the One is present to every part of ousia, to the conclusion that the One must be divided (144d), and that ousia has been divided into not more parts than the One.
limits: |
  This observation records the structure of the one-over-many reasoning at 144c-144d. It does not assess whether Parmenides endorses this argument or whether it succeeds. It does not claim this is the same argument delivered elsewhere in Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0116
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 144c-144d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 144c-144d
  start_marker: 144c
  end_marker: 144d
  start_char: 38160
  end_char: 39018
  text_sha256: a36b23e54b0acf1995640095e9889891b5a3b7e692f86e91b12edaec021aadd5
greek_terms:
  - μεμερισμένον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 144c-d Parmenides asks whether the One, being one, can be simultaneously whole in many places. The respondent (Aristoteles) examines the question and answers that it is impossible. Parmenides then draws the consequence that the One must be divided, since otherwise it could not be present to all the parts of ousia at once.
textual_basis: |
  Parmenides poses the question at 144c-d: "Is the One, being one, simultaneously whole in many places? Consider this." Aristoteles replies: "I am considering it and I see that it is impossible." Parmenides then concludes: "Divided then, if indeed not whole," and draws out the consequence.
limits: |
  This observation records the reductio structure at 144c-d. It does not claim the argument is valid or that the premise about wholeness in many places is accepted outside this dialectical exercise. It does not assess the metaphysical merits of the conclusion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0117

greek_terms:
  - πλεῖστα μέρη
  - οὐδὲ γὰρ πλείω τοῦ ἑνὸς νενέμηται
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 144d Parmenides overturns the earlier claim from 144c that ousia has the most parts. He states: "We were not speaking the truth just now when we said that ousia has been distributed into most parts. For it has been distributed into not more parts than the One."
textual_basis: |
  At 144c the argument began from "ousia has unlimited parts" and "most parts." By 144d Parmenides states explicitly that the earlier statement is not true, because ousia has been divided into not more parts than the One. This is a direct reversal within the same argumentative sequence.
limits: |
  This observation records the internal reversal of a claim within the argument at 144c-d. It does not assess whether the reversal is a genuine self-correction, a dialectical move, or an intentional paradox. It does not interpret this as commentary on the theory of forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0118

  - ἐν ἄλλῳ

observation: "At 145d-145e, an argument establishes that the One must be both in itself and in another. The reasoning proceeds through the part-whole relationship of the One: qua whole it is in another, qua all the parts it is in itself. The conclusion at 145e presents this as a necessary dual relation."
textual_basis: "The movement from the premise about the whole not being in a single part, nor in some parts, nor in all parts, forces the conclusion that the whole, if not in itself, must be in another (145d-145e). The final line at 145e explicitly states the duality: qua whole, it is in another; qua all parts, it is in itself — and thus the One must be both in itself and in another."
limits: This observation records the relational opposition structure of the argument, not whether the conclusion is ultimately affirmed or refuted in the larger dialectical exercise of the Parmenides. It does not assess the validity of the part-whole reasoning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 146e Parmenides establishes that the other is never in the same thing, then infers at 146e-147a that the one and the not-one are not other by the other. The denial that not-ones are other than each other is explicitly grounded in the phrase 'not participating in the other' (mē metechonta tou heterou) at 146e-147a.
limits: This observation records the deduction's reliance on participation language; it does not claim that the passage endorses or rejects participation as a metaphysical doctrine, nor does it assess whether the argument is sound.
```

```yaml
observation: Parmenides derives that the not-ones cannot be number, because if they had number they would somehow be one, at 147a.
textual_basis: At 147a, after Aristoteles assents that things not one do not participate in the one (otherwise they would somehow be one), Parmenides concludes 'nor would the not-ones be number' (oud' an arithmos eiē ara ta mē hen), linking the possession of number (arithmon ge echonta) to participation in the one.
limits: This records the deduction step within the Parmenides exercise; it does not assert that Plato endorses this account of number or that this exhausts the treatment of number in the dialogue.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0123

greek_terms:
  - εἰ ἄρα πάντῃ τὸ μὲν ἕν ἐστι
  - μετεῖχεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 147a Parmenides uses the conditional "if then altogether the One is" to open a new branch of the hypothesis after the preceding deduction about the not-ones has closed.
textual_basis: The 146e-147a span completes the claim that the not-ones neither are other than each other nor participate in the One. At the end of 147a, the conditional formula shifts the inquiry to a new phase.
limits: This records the structural transition; it does not interpret the content of the new hypothesis branch that follows this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0125
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 146c-146d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 146c-146d
  start_marker: 146c
  end_marker: 146d
  start_char: 42507
  end_char: 43439
  text_sha256: f7f6150ff1934168b37c802ad9daaf281e19ce3521c449361a823d63a94eda3d
greek_terms:
  - ἕτερον
  - ταὐτὸν
  - ἓν
  - ἐναντία
english_gloss: ""
observation: The argument at 146c-146d forces a contradiction by first deducing that the one must be the same as itself from the rejection of otherness, wholeness, and partness (146c), and then immediately deriving that it must be other than itself from the earlier admission that it is in another (146c-146d). The two conclusions — same as itself and other than itself — are presented as a necessary opposed pair.
textual_basis: At 146c the interrogator asks whether, if the one is neither other than, nor a whole of, nor a part in relation to itself, it must necessarily be the same as itself. Then at 146c-146d the interrogator appeals to the earlier appearance that the one is both in itself and in another to conclude that the one would be other than itself in this respect.
limits: Does not claim that the argument is sophistical or that Parmenides intends a paradox; only records the observable structure of forced contradictory derivation from previously admitted premises.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0126
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 146c-146d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 146c-146d
  start_marker: 146c
  end_marker: 146d
  start_char: 42507
  end_char: 43439
  text_sha256: f7f6150ff1934168b37c802ad9daaf281e19ce3521c449361a823d63a94eda3d
greek_terms:
  - ταὐτὸν
  - ἕτερον
  - ἐναντία
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 146d the argument explicitly introduces the opposition of sameness and otherness as contraries and draws a consequence about the impossibility of one being in the other. The opposition is treated as a formal principle governing participation: the other cannot ever be in the same, with the result that there is no being in which the other is present for any time."
textual_basis: "At 146d the interrogator says \"Look: aren't the same itself and the other contrary to one another?\" and then asks whether the same will ever consent to be in the other or the other in the same. When the answer is no, the conclusion follows that if the other will never be in the same, there is no being in which the other is present for any time at all."
limits: Does not claim that this is a doctrine of Forms; records only the oppositional deployment of sameness and otherness within the dialectical exercise. Does not evaluate whether the move is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 148a-b, the argument constructs an exhaustive opposition between being different and being same, then derives contrary likeness-predicates from each. The One is shown to be both like in virtue of being different from the others, and unlike in virtue of being the same as the others.
textual_basis: The text explicitly states that like is contrary to unlike (148a) and different is contrary to same (148a). From these contraries, the argument forces the conclusion that the One is both like and unlike the others (148b).
limits: This records the forced alternative structure of the argument; it does not evaluate whether the derivation is valid or whether the Parmenides dialogue endorses this result.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0131
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 147b-147c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  start_char: 44277
  end_char: 45154
  text_sha256: f55c18dbfef7561477787f61a7a48052571e8160c78a11c0f6ef74e631b8a52e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "From the prior finding that the one is different from the others, the argument infers that the others must also be different from the one, and then restricts that difference to a symmetrical relation: the one is different from the others in the same way the others are different from the one, neither more nor less."
textual_basis: At 147b the argument recapitulates the agreed premises about parts, wholes, and sameness, then concludes that the one is different from the others and from itself and the same as them and itself. At 147c a new step qualifies the difference claim with the formula of neither more nor less, yielding the further conclusion that the one and the others are alike in respect of being different.
limits: The observation records the procedural move of qualifying a conclusion by symmetry and equality of relation. It does not assess whether the argument for the initial sameness-and-difference conclusion is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

The argument then builds on this recalled premise to reach the sameness-and-difference result.
```yaml
textual_basis: The phrase marking recall of prior agreements at 147b reintroduces previously established premises about parts, wholes, and otherness. The conclusion follows from applying these recalled premises to the one and the not-one.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0133
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 147d-147e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 147d-147e
  start_marker: 147d
  end_marker: 147e
  start_char: 45154
  end_char: 45874
  text_sha256: bfdd44077a8a475f8e2115ebe552409ba1c9a624afe8632066b28816d95b63e9
greek_terms:
  - ἕτερον
  - ὄνομα
english_gloss: the different, name, nature
observation: |
  The argument establishes a principle that a name, whether uttered once or many times, always refers to the same nature and does not shift reference. This is then applied to the term "different" (heteron): when it is said twice, it still designates the same nature, not two distinct referents.
textual_basis: |
  At 147d-e, Parmenides secures agreement that each name is called "upon something" (epi tini), that the same name uttered many times always says the same thing, and that the name "different" does the same. The conclusion at 147e: when we say "the others are different from the one" and "the one is different from the others," saying "different" twice does not apply it to a different nature; it always names that nature of which it is the name.
limits: |
  This is a procedural principle about naming consistency, not a metaphysical claim about Forms. It sets a rule for the deductive exercise that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0134
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 147d-147e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 147d-147e
  start_marker: 147d
  end_marker: 147e
  start_char: 45154
  end_char: 45874
  text_sha256: bfdd44077a8a475f8e2115ebe552409ba1c9a624afe8632066b28816d95b63e9
greek_terms:
  - πολλὴ ἀνάγκη
  - ἀνάγκη
english_gloss: great necessity, necessity
observation: |
  Parmenides leads the respondent through a rapid assent chain establishing the naming principle: each name is applied to something, the same name said many times always says the same thing, and the name "different" follows the same rule. The respondent gives short assents throughout.
textual_basis: |
  At 147d, the exchange proceeds: "How do you mean?" — "Thus: do you call each name upon something?" — "I do." — "Would you say the same name more than once?" — "I would." — "Whether you say it once or many times, does great necessity compel you to say the same thing always?" — "Certainly." At 147e the necessity language recurs with "anankê."
limits: |
  This is a local sequence within the deductions of the second part of the Parmenides, not an elenctic refutation of a definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0135
source_work: Parmenides

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  start_marker: 147d
  end_marker: 147e

greek_terms:
  - ἕτερον
  - φύσει
english_gloss: different, by nature
observation: |
  The argument equates the reference of a name with "that nature" (ekeinê tê phusei) of which it is the name, treating the name "different" as anchored to a single, stable nature rather than to multiple instances of usage. This is applied argumentatively: saying "different" twice does not multiply the nature named.
textual_basis: |
  At 147e, Parmenides states: when we say "the others are different from the one" and "the one is different from the others," saying "different" twice we do not apply it to a different nature but always to that nature of which it was the name (ouk ep' allêi… all' ep' ekeinê tê phusei auto aei legomen hêsper ên tounoma).
limits: |
  The passage deploys a naming-to-nature principle inside a deductive exercise about the one. It does not develop a full theory of Forms or name-nature correspondence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: At 148c-148d the argument derives that the One is both like and unlike the others, and then both like and unlike itself, by applying the prior results that the One is both same as (tauton) and different from (heteron) the others, and likewise in relation to itself.
textual_basis: "The speaker explicitly marks the prior results as grounds at 148c: 'since it appeared to be both same as the others and different' (tauton te ara on to hen tois allois kai hoti heteron esti). The conclusion is reached by both criteria together and each separately (kat' amphotera kai kata hekateron). The same move is then applied reflexively to the One's relation to itself, citing the earlier finding that the One appeared different from itself and same as itself."
limits: This observation notes the argument's reliance on prior deductions; it does not assess whether the definitions of likeness and unlikeness used here are sound or whether the sameness/difference premises were correctly established.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 148d the argument transitions from the topic of likeness and unlikeness to the topic of contact (tou haptesthai), marked by an explicit procedural question.
textual_basis: The transition begins at 148d with 'ti de de; peri tou haptesthai to hen hautou kai ton allon kai tou me haptesthai peri pos echei, skopei' ('What then? Concerning the One touching itself and the others and not touching — how does it stand? Consider it.'). This formula introduces a new subject of inquiry while the respondent signals readiness ('skopo').
limits: This observation identifies the transition marker; it does not analyze the content of the contact argument that follows or its relationship to the likeness/unlikeness results.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0141
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 146a-146b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

greek_terms:
  - ταὐτόν
  - ἕτερον
  - κινεῖσθαί
  - ἑστάναι
english_gloss: same, other/different, moved, at rest
observation: |
  At 146a, the argument deduces that the One must be both always in motion and always at rest, because it is always in itself and always in another.  The conclusion that the same entity is characterized by both opposing predicates—motion and rest—is presented as a necessary consequence of the premises, producing a forced alternative.
textual_basis: |
  The passage reasons: if the One is always in itself it must be at rest; if it is always in another it must be in motion.  The conclusion is explicitly marked as necessary.  This is an instance of the forced alternative pattern where the respondent is compelled to accept both of two opposed predicates.
limits: |
  The observation notes the formal structure of forced opposed predicate attribution.  It does not claim that this is a contradiction that invalidates the hypothesis, or that the argument is intended to be a genuine refutation rather than a dialectical exercise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The text explicitly applies the spatial premise to self-touch at 148e and the segment ends by indicating that the condition for self-touch would make the One two at 148e.
```

```yaml

  - ἐφεξῆς
  - ἅψις
  - ἅψεις
english_gloss: touch, touches, one
observation: At 149a-149b Parmenides defines touch by separation, adjacency, and the absence of a third item between, then derives the count rule that touches are always one fewer than the number of contiguous terms.
textual_basis: "At 149a the touching item must be separate from and next to what it touches, with no third between them, so at least two items are required for touch. At 149b the addition of a third term yields two touches, and the rule is generalized: each added term adds one touch, leaving touches one fewer than the number of terms."
limits: This observation records the definition-and-count sequence for touch. It does not repeat the preceding self-touch denial or assess whether the count rule applies outside this dialectical exercise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 149e-150a the argument explicitly introduces two forms (eide), largeness and smallness, as entities that are opposites of each other and can be present in (engignesthai) beings. Their existence is argued from the fact that they are opposites and are present in beings.
textual_basis: |
  Parmenides 149e states that these are two forms, largeness and smallness, and argues that if they did not exist they could neither be opposites of each other nor be present in beings (en tois ousin engignoisthen).
limits: |
  This observation notes the introduction of these two named forms within the dialectical exercise of the second hypothesis. It does not assess whether these forms correspond to the theory of forms found in middle-period dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: |
  At 150a, when considering how smallness could be present in (engignetai) the One, the argument poses the exclusive alternative that it must be either in the whole (en holo) or in a part (en merei) of the One. This is a recurrence of the whole/part participation dilemma.
textual_basis: |
  Parmenides 150a states the conditional: if smallness comes to be in the One, it would be either in the whole or in a part of it (etoi en holo an e en merei autou eneie).
limits: |
  This observation identifies the structural recurrence of the whole/part dilemma within the second hypothesis. It does not include the preceding introduction of largeness and smallness as Forms, which is recorded nearby.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0147
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 150d-150e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 150d-150e
  start_marker: 150d
  end_marker: 150e
  start_char: 51547
  end_char: 52329
  text_sha256: 305bef2764760492d89199123beebf5296138d7207fc4385cca60306c8560993
greek_terms:
  - μήτε μέγεθος μήτε σμικρότητα
  - ὑπερέχειν
  - ὑπερέχεσθαι
  - ἐξ ἴσου
  - ἴσον
english_gloss: The One has neither largeness nor smallness, so it neither exceeds nor is exceeded, and being on an equality it is equal to itself and to the others.
observation: "At 150d-150e, Parmenides constructs a deductive chain from the premise that the One has neither largeness (megethos) nor smallness (smikroteta) to the conclusion that it is equal to itself and to the others. The chain proceeds: if the One is neither larger nor smaller than the others, then necessarily it neither exceeds nor is exceeded; what neither exceeds nor is exceeded is on an equality (ex isou) and therefore equal (ison). The same reasoning is applied to the One's relation to itself."
textual_basis: "The argument in 150d-150e uses a short question-and-assent sequence: 'if the One is neither greater nor less than the others, must it not that it neither exceeds them nor is exceeded by them?' — 'Necessarily.' — 'And that which neither exceeds nor is exceeded must of necessity be on an equality, and being on an equality, equal.' — 'How could it not?' This is a classic assent chain where each step is confirmed before building the next."
limits: This observation records the assent-chain structure of the equality deduction. It does not evaluate whether the premise (that the One lacks magnitude attributes) is itself sound or whether the One's equality with itself and with the others is consistent with earlier deductions in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0148
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 150d-150e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 150d-150e
  start_marker: 150d
  end_marker: 150e
  start_char: 51547
  end_char: 52329
  text_sha256: 305bef2764760492d89199123beebf5296138d7207fc4385cca60306c8560993
greek_terms:
  - ἑαυτῷ
  - πρὸς ἀλλήλω
english_gloss: "The equality conclusion is applied in two registers: the One's relation to itself (pros heauto/heautoi) and its relation to the others (pros allelo)."
observation: "At 150d-150e, Parmenides first deduces that the One is equal to the others, then extends the same reasoning by an explicit parallel step (kai men kai auto ge to hen pros heauto) to conclude that the One is also equal to itself, using the same premise that it has neither largeness nor smallness (mete megethos en heautoi mete smikroteta echon). The conclusion is then stated as a consolidated thesis: the One would be equal to itself and to the others."
textual_basis: "The passage at 150d-150e runs the same deduction twice: first the One's equality to the others (oukoun to ge mete hyperechon mete hyperechomenon polle ananke ex isou einai... ison einai), then with an explicit structural marker 'and further' (kai men kai auto ge to hen pros heauto) the same logic is applied to the One's self-relation, yielding 'it would be equal to itself' (ison an eie heautoi). The conclusion (to hen ara heautoi te kai tois allois ison an eie) synthesizes both registers."
limits: This observation notes the structural extension of a deduction from the other-relation to the self-relation. It does not assess whether the self-relation step introduces any new logical commitments or whether the pairing of self-equality and other-equality is ritical to the Parmenidean exercise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - κοινωνία
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Parmenides proposes a definition of "being" as participation in ousia with present time, and distinguishes it from "was" as community with ousia along with past time, and "will be" as community with ousia along with future time.
textual_basis: |
  At 151e-152a, Parmenides asks whether being is anything other than participation in ousia with present time, just as "was" is community with ousia along with past time and "will be" with future time. The interlocutor assents, and the definition is then used to establish that the One participates in time.
limits: |
  This is a local definition within the second hypothesis' dialectical exercise about the One; it does not commit Plato to this temporal analysis of being as a general metaphysical doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  At 152a-152b the questions move from participation in time to becoming older than itself, then to the rule that the older is older than something younger, and finally to the result that the One becomes both older and younger than itself. Each step is confirmed by short respondent assents.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0154
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 150b-150c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 150b-150c
  start_marker: 150b
  end_marker: 150c
  start_char: 50725
  end_char: 51547
  text_sha256: c1518bb3906089f8ed7d9598dd7961d360caa2a28acad558b0777f210fa83115
greek_terms:
  - σμικρότης
observation: |
  The argument at 150b concludes that Smallness cannot be present in any being, whether in a whole or in a part, because if it were in all of a part it would be equal to or larger than that part. Therefore nothing is small except Smallness itself.
textual_basis: |
  The deduction moves through an assent chain: Smallness cannot be in the whole One (150b), and if at all only in a part; but not in all of a part either, for it would then be equal to or larger than that part (150b). The conclusion follows: Smallness will never be present in any being, neither in part nor in whole, and nothing will be small except Smallness itself (150b).
limits: |
  This observation records the whole-part structure of the argument and its conclusion that the form alone is what it names. It does not assess whether this conclusion is consistent with other Parmenides passages or with claims about participation elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 151c the respondent answers 'pos d' ou' to the move from measures to parts, then 'houtos' to the correlation of greater/lesser/equal with measures and parts. At 151d 'phainetai' assents to the numerical consequence for the One in relation to itself, and 'ananke' assents to the same pattern applied to the One's relation to the others.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0157
source_work: Parmenides

  text_sha256: f00f00e9eddafbfba5b7c5677f28afa79d06360ba739cd31698034c79a86db85
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The argument at 151c-151d proceeds by a chain of conditional steps linking the One's magnitude relations (greater/lesser/equal) to measures (metra), then to parts (mere), and finally to number (arithmon), using 'epeide de' (since) and 'oukoun' (therefore) to mark each inferential move.
textual_basis: "At 151c the argument moves from measures to parts via 'epeide de metron, kai meron' (since of measures, also of parts). At 151d the same linkage is repeated: 'epeide de metron, kai meron.' The final step moves from equal/fewer/more parts to equal/lesser/greater number (arithmon). The 'oukoun' at 151d then applies the identical reasoning to the One's relation to the others with 'hosautos.'"
limits: This observation notes the conditional-deductive structure of the argument. It does not evaluate the validity of the measure-part-number inference, nor does it claim anything about the metaphysical significance of the One having number.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0159
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 152d-152e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 152d-152e
  start_marker: 152d
  end_marker: 152e
  start_char: 55746
  end_char: 56742
  text_sha256: b492720b6169c97ce27da504499c325a7a4c312562a1abd7be83909b811d2168
greek_terms:
  - νῦν
  - ἀεὶ
  - πάρεστι
  - τῷ ἑνὶ
english_gloss: now, always, is present to, to the One
observation: |
  At 152e the argument presents a forced alternative: the One must either spend more time being than becoming, or equal time, with respect to itself. The interlocutor selects equal time, and from this equal-time premise it follows that the One is neither older nor younger than itself, but the earlier conclusion that it both is and becomes older and younger than itself remains. The two conclusions stand in tension without resolution, producing a typical elenctic aporia.
textual_basis: |
  At 152e the text asks whether the One spends more time being than becoming, or equal time. The respondent selects equal time. The conclusion that follows is that the One, spending equal time being and becoming relative to itself, is neither younger nor older than itself nor does it become so. This directly contradicts the earlier claim at 152e (immediately above) that the One is and becomes older than itself and younger than itself. The respondent's final comment of doubt and the inability to answer about the others underscore the unresolved tension.
limits: |
  This observation does not claim that the contradiction is deliberately left unresolved for esoteric reasons. It merely notes the structure: a forced choice between alternatives leads to a conclusion that contradicts a prior conclusion, and the respondent registers this, which is a recurring pattern in the Parmenides' dialectical examination.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0160
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 152b-152c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 152b-152c
  start_marker: 152b
  end_marker: 152c
  start_char: 55036
  end_char: 55746
  text_sha256: b28f30d8f4452555b7ce122d0d1e4cfe521f71dd7bc6ccc330cdc4749b4094e6
greek_terms:
  - γίγνεται
  - πρεσβύτερον
  - νῦν
english_gloss: comes-to-be, older, now
observation: |
  At 152b-152c, the argument examines whether the one becomes older than itself, introducing the now as a temporal limit that must be met for the becoming of older to crystallize into being. The text states that what proceeds forward cannot overleap the now, and when it encounters the now it stops becoming older and instead already is older.
textual_basis: |
  The passage asserts that a thing becomes older or younger as time advances, then poses the condition that it is older only when, in respect of the now, it comes to be between the was and the will be. When it meets the now it ceases becoming and is at that moment older. The now thus serves as a punctual limit marking the transition from process to state.
limits: |
  The observation records the function attributed to the now in the temporal argument at 152b-c; it does not assess whether this account of the now is consistent with other Platonic treatments or whether Parmenides endorses it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0161
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 152b-152c

greek_terms:
  - νῦν
  - ἔπειτα
english_gloss: proceeding, now, thereafter
observation: |
  At 152c, the argument characterizes the proceeding thing as touching both the now and the thereafter: it releases the now and seizes the thereafter, coming to be between both. This gives the now a Janus-faced role, simultaneously bound to past release and future grasp.
textual_basis: |
  The text states that what proceeds has the character of laying hold of both the now and the thereafter, letting go of the now and taking hold of the thereafter, and coming to be between both. The now is thus not only a limit but also a point of departure into the future within the process of becoming.
limits: |
  The observation describes the spatial-temporal image deployed in the argument; it does not evaluate whether this image commits the argument to a paradox or whether Parmenides treats the image as merely dialectical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0165

greek_terms:
  - παρὰ φύσιν
  - πρεσβύτερον

english_gloss: contrary to nature, older, younger, simultaneously
observation: "At 153e the argument introduces a conditional safeguard: if the One itself is not of a nature contrary to nature, then it comes to be neither earlier nor later than the others but simultaneously. The inference from a common age to simultaneity of generation is made to depend on this nature-condition."
textual_basis: The passage moves from the claim that the One shares the same age as all others to the conclusion that it is neither older nor younger (153e-154a). But the conclusion is explicitly qualified by the hypothesis about the One's nature, marking a conditional step in the argument chain.
limits: This record notes the presence of a hypothesis condition, not whether the condition is satisfied or what it implies about the One's essence. It does not assess the validity of the inference.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 151b Parmenides draws the explicit conclusion that the One is equal to, larger than, and smaller than both itself and the Others. This follows a chain of reasoning about mutual containment between the One and the Others at 151a-151b, where the One is in the Others (making it smaller because contained) and the Others are in the One (making it larger because containing).
limits: This observation records the logical conclusion as stated. It does not assess whether the conclusion is meant to be genuinely accepted or whether it reveals a flaw in the preceding premises. The broader dialectical purpose is not determined here.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0168
source_work: Parmenides

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  start_marker: 151a
  end_marker: 151b
  start_char: 52329
  end_char: 53283
  text_sha256: 40e4f7801142c049d32015b76471e1ccbd18dc87f2b9b827dabdd11f620d4625
greek_terms:
  - περιέχοντα
  - περιεχόμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The argument at 151a-151b derives opposite size relations (larger, smaller) from the same relational property of mutual containment: the One is contained by the Others and therefore smaller, but also contains the Others and is therefore larger. The conclusion applies the same relational logic to the One relative to itself."
textual_basis: "At 151a the reasoning establishes that because nothing exists outside the One and the Others, they must be in each other. From this single relation of mutual containment, two opposed conclusions follow: the One is smaller than the Others (because contained) and the One is larger than the Others (because containing them). The same logic is then applied to the One in relation to itself at 151b."
limits: This observation records the argumentative structure of deriving contrary predicates from a single relational premise. It does not evaluate whether the mutual-containment premise is sound or whether the argument exploits an equivocation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - οὐδέποτε
english_gloss: ""
observation: The dialectical chain at 154d-154e culminates in a paradoxical conclusion that the younger thing never is older but always becomes older relative to the older thing, distinguishing being from becoming in temporal relations.
textual_basis: At 154e the argument concludes that the younger thing "esti de oudepote presbyteron, alla gignetai aei ekeinou presbyteron" — it never is older but always becomes older. The paradox turns on the simultaneity of the younger becoming older while never reaching the state of being older, because the older likewise advances toward the younger.
limits: Does not assess whether the paradox is valid or sophistical. Records only that the elenchus produces a paradoxical conclusion through an assent chain, not what Plato intends by it.
review_status: accepted
```

The answer "smikroteroi" (by a smaller fraction) drives the conclusion that the proportional age-difference between the One and the Others continuously shrinks, producing the paradox at 154e.'


```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0177
source_work: Parmenides

greek_terms:
  - χρόνος δέ γε οὐδεὶς ἔστιν
  - μεταβάλλει
  - ἑστὸς
  - κινούμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 156c Parmenides argues that what changes from rest to motion or from motion to rest must be in no time at all, since no time can contain a thing that is simultaneously neither moving nor at rest.
textual_basis: The 156c argument says that the changing thing must be in no time whatever and that there is no time in which something can be neither moving nor at rest. It then excludes change while the thing is either at rest or in motion.
limits: This observation records the no-time transition claim for motion and rest. It does not include the subsequent account of the instant, which is recorded separately.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0178
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 155c-155d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 155c-155d
  start_marker: 155c
  end_marker: 155d
  start_char: 61830
  end_char: 62709
  text_sha256: afe663f2eaf3b0f4ec850d693fc4e10527769b5898c24e2caa745ab7db10a5d5
greek_terms:
  - πρεσβύτερον
  - νεώτερον
  - χρόνου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 155c the argument refines the sense in which the one can be both older and younger than the others (and itself) by tying the opposition to temporal process: the one becomes older than the others in that the earlier becomes later and the later becomes earlier in time, yet also neither is nor becomes older or younger."
textual_basis: The text explicitly states that when things that are earlier become later and things that are later become earlier (ta protera tōn hysteron genomena kai ta hystera tōn proterōn), by that very fact they must become older and younger than each other. The passage then affirms both that the one is and becomes older and younger and that it neither is nor becomes older or younger (155c).
limits: This observation records the refinement of the older/younger opposition within the temporal argument; it does not claim that the contradiction is resolved or that Plato endorses the conclusion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0179
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 155c-155d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 155c-155d
  start_marker: 155c
  end_marker: 155d
  start_char: 61830
  end_char: 62709
  text_sha256: afe663f2eaf3b0f4ec850d693fc4e10527769b5898c24e2caa745ab7db10a5d5
greek_terms:
  - ἔπειτα
  - νῦν
  - ἦν
  - ἔστι
  - ἔσται
  - ἐγίγνετο
  - γίγνεται
  - γενήσεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 155c-d, once the one has been shown to participate in time (chronou metechei), the argument infers that all temporal modes apply to it: past, present and future existence (ēn kai esti kai estai), and past, present and future becoming (egineto kai gignetai kai genēsetai)."
textual_basis: The text asks whether, if the one participates in time and in becoming older and younger, it must also participate in the past (pote), the future (epeita), and the present (nun). Upon assent, it concludes with the sixfold list of temporal predicates covering both being and becoming across all three times (155c-d).
limits: This observation records the inference from time-participation to full temporal modality; it does not assess whether the inference is valid or whether the conclusion conflicts with earlier Parmenidean claims about the one.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0180
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 155c-155d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 155c-155d
  start_marker: 155c
  end_marker: 155d
  start_char: 61830
  end_char: 62709
  text_sha256: afe663f2eaf3b0f4ec850d693fc4e10527769b5898c24e2caa745ab7db10a5d5
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμη
  - δόξα
  - αἴσθησις
  - ὄνομα
  - λόγος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 155d the argument extends the temporal conclusion to epistemological and linguistic predicates: since the one participates in time and the interlocutors are even now performing acts of knowledge (epistēmē), opinion (doxa), and perception (aisthēsis) about it, there can be knowledge, opinion, perception, a name (onoma), and an account (logos) of the one."
textual_basis: The text argues that if the one participates in time, there would be knowledge, opinion and perception of it, on the grounds that even now the interlocutors are performing all these activities concerning it (epeidē kai nun hēmeis peri autou panta tauta prattomen). It then adds that the one has a name and an account (155d).
limits: This observation records the extension of the temporal argument into epistemological and linguistic registers; it does not claim that the conclusion is Plato's own view or that it resolves the paradox.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0181

english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 154b-c, Parmenides distinguishes between being older/younger and becoming older/younger. The One is older than the Others and the Others are younger than the One, but neither becomes older or younger. Static temporal predicates are affirmed while the process of coming to be those predicates is denied.
textual_basis: |
  The text states at 154c that the One is and has become older while the Other is younger, but becoming is denied. The argument at 154b-c turns on the difference between static being and process.
limits: |
  This observation notes a textual distinction between being and becoming in temporal predicates. It does not assess the philosophical validity of the distinction or its relation to broader Parmenidean ontology.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - προστιθέμενα
  - διαφέρειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 154b, Parmenides deploys a mathematical principle as a deductive premise: unequal things to which equal things are added always differ by the same amount as they first differed. From this premise and the respondent's assent, Parmenides concludes that the One cannot become older or younger than the Others.
textual_basis: |
  The principle is stated at 154b: unequal things plus equal additions preserve the original difference. The respondent assents fully at 154b. The conclusion follows at 154b-c that the One does not become older or younger.
limits: |
  This observation identifies the argument structure and the use of an arithmetic premise in a dialectical deduction. It does not evaluate whether the premise correctly applies to temporal relations or whether the conclusion is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0184

observation: At 156a the argument treats coming-to-be and perishing as taking on (metalambanein) and departing from (apallattesthai) being (ousia), linking temporal change to participation in being through distinct temporal moments.
textual_basis: "At 156a the exchange defines coming-to-be as partaking of being and perishing as departing from being: 'indeed, do you not call partaking of being coming-to-be?' and 'departing from being is it not perishing?'. The one is then said to take on and release being."
limits: This records the presence of participation vocabulary applied to temporal becoming, not a general doctrine of participation. It covers only the terms as used in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0185
source_work: Parmenides

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 155e a temporal exclusivity argument forces the conclusion that the one partakes of being at one time and does not partake at another time, excluding the possibility of simultaneous participation and non-participation.
textual_basis: "At 155e the argument asks: when the one partakes, can it then not partake? Or when it does not partake, can it partake? The answer is 'not possible'. From this it is concluded that the one partakes in one time and does not partake in another, for only in that way could it both partake and not partake of the same thing. This is a forced alternative excluding mixed states."
limits: This records a logical structure of forced alternatives within the Parmenidean deduction. It does not evaluate the soundness of the argument or its implications for the theory of forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0186
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 157e-158a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 157e-158a
  start_marker: 157e
  end_marker: 158a
  start_char: 67240
  end_char: 68035
  text_sha256: 2f678d1c8e3b77254761bea9aab37204c6f655660aa9fabd03bfd2ed3314fae1
greek_terms:
  - μετέχοι
  - μετέχειν
  - μετεῖχεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 157e-158a, the argument chains together multiple participation claims: the others participate in the one (157e), each part must participate in the one (158a), and both the whole and the part participate in the one (158a). The verb metechein and its cognates appear at least four times across this brief span as the central logical operator.
textual_basis: |
  The passage moves from "if the others have parts, they would participate in the whole and the one" (157e), to "each part must participate in the one" (158a), to the counterfactual "otherwise it would not participate but would be the one itself" (158a), concluding that both whole and part participate (158a).
limits: |
  This observation records the density of participation vocabulary in a deductive context. It does not evaluate whether the argument is valid or whether the participation relation is consistent across the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἄλλο ὂν ἢ ἕν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 158a, the argument asserts that being one is impossible for anything except the one itself. The others cannot be the one; they can only participate in the one while being other than one. This distinguishes being the form from participating in the form.
textual_basis: |
  The text states "now being one is impossible, I think, except for the one itself" (158a) and explains that the part "would participate in the one, obviously being other than one; for otherwise it would not participate but would itself be one" (158a).
limits: |
  This observation notes a strict exclusivity claim about the form: only the form itself can be what it is. It does not assess whether this claim is compatible with other passages in the Parmenides or other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  At 158d-e, the dialectical deduction deploys participation vocabulary (metechei) to describe the relation between "the others" (ta alla) and limit: the others are said to "participate in limit" (peratos metexei), extending the participation lexicon to the opposition of limit and unlimitedness within the others.
textual_basis: |
  Parmenides states at 158d that the others are both unlimited (apeira) and participate in limit (peratos metexei). The verb metechei is used to characterize how limit belongs to the others alongside their own nature of unlimitedness.
limits: |
  The observation registers the presence of metechei vocabulary in a dialectical context; it does not assert a developed theory of participation, nor does it claim that the usage here is identical to participation language in other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0189
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 158d-158e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 158d-158e
  start_marker: 158d
  end_marker: 158e

  end_char: 69689
  text_sha256: a9c5474abd0ac192e29b402fd42a910ecb201f3a57f5eaf76311959732559ba4
greek_terms:
  - ὅμοιά τε καὶ ἀνόμοια
  - ταὐτὸν πεπονθότα
  - ἐναντία πάθη
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 158e, the same subjects (the others) are affirmed to be both like (homoia) and unlike (anomoia) each other and themselves. The dual predication is sustained by distinguishing two respects: insofar as they are all unlimited by their own nature, they share the same condition (tauton peponthota) and are alike; insofar as they are both limited and unlimited, they possess contrary attributes (enantia pathe) and are unlike.
textual_basis: |
  The text introduces the question of likeness and unlikeness at 158e (oukoun kai homoia te kai anomoia allelois te kai heautois). Parmenides then argues hei men... apeira... tauton peponthota an eie tautei (insofar as unlimited, same condition, alike) and hei de ge peperasmena te einai kai apeira peponthen, enantia pathe... (insofar as both limited and unlimited, contrary attributes, unlike).
limits: |
  The observation records the argumentative structure of affirming opposing predicates through aspect-distinction; it does not evaluate the validity of the reasoning or claim that this pattern is unique to this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0190

  text_sha256: 540d23d9f6be25933a0289ba96b781788d188de6719a2d67007956fd9e3cce64
greek_terms:
  - τὸ ἐξαίφνης
  - μεταξὺ
  - κινήσεώς τε καὶ στάσεως
english_gloss: ""
observation: The instant (to exaiphnes) is introduced as a strange/absurd nature (atopos tis physis) that sits between (metaxy) motion and rest, serving as the point from which change proceeds into either opposite state.
textual_basis: At 156d, Parmenides names the instant (to exaiphnes) and defines it as a strange nature that sits between motion and rest (atopos tis enkathetai metaxy tes kineseos te kai staseos). At 156e, it is described as the point from which and into which the moving changes to rest and the resting changes to motion (eis tauten de kai ek tautes to te kinoumenon metaballei epi to hestanai kai to hestos epi to kineisthai).
limits: This observation records the definition of the instant as an intermediate concept between opposed states. It does not assess whether the instant is itself a form, whether the argument is valid, or whether Plato endorses this concept outside the Parmenides.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0191

greek_terms:
  - τὸ ἐξαίφνης
  - ἐν χρόνῳ οὐδενὶ οὖσα
  - ἐν οὐδενὶ χρόνῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The instant (to exaiphnes) is characterized as existing in no time (en chrono oudeni ousa, 156e), and the One, when it changes via the instant, is said to be in no time (en oudeni chrono an eie, 156e), neither moving nor resting at that moment.
textual_basis: "At 156d-e, the instant is described as being in no time (en chrono oudeni ousa). At 156e, the argument applies this to the One: when the One changes, it changes via the instant, and at that moment it is in no time (en oudeni chrono an eie), neither moving nor resting (oude kinoit' an tote, oud' an staie)."
limits: This observation records the claim that the instant and the One during change exist in no time. It does not evaluate whether this claim is coherent, whether it applies to all kinds of change, or how it relates to the broader metaphysics of time in the Parmenides.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 159a, the others (ta alla) are recapitulated as having all opposite affections: like and unlike themselves and each other, same and different, moving and at rest — summed as "all the opposite affections" (panta ta enantia pathe).
textual_basis: |
  The text at 159a enumerates "like and unlike" (homoia te kai anomoia), "same and different" (tauta de kai hetera), "moving and at rest" (kinoumena kai hestota), and concludes with "all the opposite affections" (panta ta enantia pathe). This is a compressed recapitulation of the preceding dialectical results about the others in relation to the one.
limits: |
  This observation records the recapitulation structure and its catalogue of opposite pairs. It does not assess the validity of the prior derivations or claim that the list is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0193
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 159a-159b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  start_marker: 159a
  end_marker: 159b
  start_char: 69689
  end_char: 70567
  text_sha256: 7c969d32a255036f3effba01be59416171cd7d143987ea9997e9463f969ae7d3
greek_terms:
  - λέγωμεν
  - ἐξ ἀρχῆς
  - ἓν εἰ ἔστι
  - χωρὶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 159b, Parmenides explicitly restarts the investigation "from the beginning" (ex arches) using the same hypothesis "if the one is" (hen ei esti), but introduces a new relational condition: the one and the others are separate (choris) from each other.
textual_basis: |
  The text says "let us speak from the beginning: if the one is, what must the others be affected with respect to the one?" (legomen de ex arches hen ei esti, ti chre ta alla tou henos peponthenai). Then it immediately proposes separation: "Is not the one separate from the others, and the others separate from the one?" (ar oun ou choris men to hen ton allon, choris de talla tou henos einai). This is a formal methodological restart with a modified premise, not a mere continuation.
limits: |
  This observation identifies the formal restart structure. It does not claim that the restart invalidates prior results; the text explicitly defers prior conclusions as "clear" (phanera) rather than rejecting them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  The phrase "if we leave these as now evident, let us examine again" (ei tauta men ede eomen hos phanera, episkopoimen de palin) at 159b explicitly defers the accumulated results as settled and shifts to a new inquiry. This is a structural transition marker, not a philosophical argument.
limits: |
  This is a structural observation about the text's organizational device. It does not claim that the deferred results are philosophically settled or final.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 161c-161d, from the participation of the hypothesized one in inequality, the argument derives that greatness and smallness belong to it, and then infers that equality must also belong to it as what lies between greatness and smallness.
textual_basis: "The argument moves in steps: the one is unequal to the others (161c), and inequality entails greatness and smallness (161d). The text then asks whether anything other than equality can be said to lie between greatness and smallness (161d), and the interlocutor answers that there is not."
limits: This observation records the structural move of deriving a mid-point concept from two opposed terms. It does not evaluate the soundness of the inference or claim that the pattern is unique to this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0197
source_work: Parmenides

greek_terms:
  - οὐκ ὄν
  - ὄν
  - δεσμὸν
  - οὐσίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: The respondent (presumably young Aristotle) gives only brief assent formulae across the entire span 161e-162a, while Parmenides delivers a dense, uninterrupted chain of deductive steps. The argument requires assent at key pivots (e.g., 'must we not also say that we speak of things that are?'), and each time the respondent concedes without resistance.
textual_basis: At 161e Parmenides poses a question about participation in ousia, and the respondent answers with a single word of agreement. Parmenides then develops the argument that if the statement 'the one is not' is true, the things spoken of must be, at 161e-162a. The respondent's only contributions are brief affirmations such as 'necessity' (ananke) and 'absolutely so' (pantapasi men oun), while Parmenides moves through multiple inferential steps without challenge.
limits: This observation notes the turn-taking pattern and does not claim the argument is coercive or that the respondent is intellectually defeated. The turn geometry is visible from the distribution of speech; the respondent's motives are not claimed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0198
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 161e-162a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 161e-162a
  start_marker: 161e
  end_marker: 162a
  start_char: 75838
  end_char: 76694
  text_sha256: ab3040e0a6e99a25f84dec685a8312ada44d74b5d897d7ecb4f620c5a7e1315a
greek_terms:
  - δεσμὸν
  - μὴ εἶναι
  - εἶναι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides uses the language of participation (metechein) throughout 161e-162a to describe the relationship of the not-being one to being, equality, largeness, and smallness, and introduces the term 'bond' (desmon) to describe the necessary linkage between being and not-being in the not-being one. The participation vocabulary is applied in a purely dialectical exercise, not in reference to Forms.
textual_basis: At 161e the not-being one is said to participate (meteien) in equality, largeness, and smallness, and Parmenides then asks whether it must also participate (metechein) in being (ousia). At 162a the reasoning culminates in the claim that the not-being one must have a bond (desmon) of being for its not-being, just as being has not-being so that it may fully be. The vocabulary of participation and the bond metaphor operate within the hypothesis 'if the one is not,' without reference to separate Forms.
limits: This observation records the presence of participation language in a non-Form context. It does not claim that the passage undermines the theory of Forms or that the dialectical exercise is meant to train for Form discourse. The observation only notes that the same vocabulary is deployed outside its canonical context.
review_status: accepted
```

No speaker intervention longer than a single assent formula occurs between the dialectical steps."

The same participation language is applied to what-is, which participates in not-being."
```yaml
textual_basis: The Greek at 162b reads to te onti tou me einai kai to me onti tou einai metesti, kai to heni, epeide ouk esti, tou einai ananke meteinai — participation in being and not-being is attributed to being, not-being, and the one.
limits: This records the presence of participation vocabulary in a dialectical deduction about the one, not about Forms. It does not claim the one is a Form or that this usage is identical to the participation language of the middle dialogues.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The Greek text at the 162c boundary reads metabole de kinesis; e ti phesomen; kinesis. The respondent immediately assents, and this premise is then used to derive that the not-being one is moving (kinoumenon).
limits: This records the explicit conceptual identification within the dialectical exercise. It does not assess whether the identification holds outside this deduction or whether it is consistent with other Platonic treatments of change and motion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  end_char: 73140
  text_sha256: 9d24cb7506856b529e7988443e0cf80175881d0465ef885832c966c3165b8407
greek_terms:
  - πᾶν τοὐναντίον
  - διαφέρει μόνον
  - πάνυ γε
english_gloss: ""
observation: Parmenides interrogates whether the negation 'one is not' differs from 'not-one is not.' The respondent first says it differs, then is asked whether it differs only or is the complete opposite. The respondent settles on 'complete opposite,' establishing a sharp logical distinction between two negative formulations before the investigation proceeds.
textual_basis: "At 160b-c Parmenides asks what the hypothesis 'one is not' would be, and whether it differs from 'not-one is not.' The respondent says it differs. Parmenides then presses: does it differ only or is it the complete opposite to say 'not-one is not' as against 'one is not'? The respondent affirms it is the complete opposite. The dialectical exchange forces a maximal distinction before investigation begins."
limits: This observation captures the procedural move of clarifying hypothesis scope. It does not assess whether the distinction is logically sustainable or how it plays out in later deductions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 160c Parmenides asserts that whoever says 'one' says something knowable and different from the others, and that the hearer knows what the speaker means.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0208
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 157c-157d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt

  end_char: 67240
  text_sha256: 8bef0bf11ed08ed60cb9818a0912ad25270b4b8fc5bbf33cd6b2949fadb9e0c4
greek_terms:
  - μόρια
  - μετέχει
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 157c-157d the argument about the Others' relation to the One shifts into mereology: the text reasons that if the Others have parts of the One they are other than the One, and that a part cannot belong to many things but must belong to one form.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 157c states that the Others participate somehow in the One because they have parts of the One. It then develops a chain (157c-157d) showing that a part cannot be a part of many things or of all things, but only of a single whole or one form (157d). This is a linked sequence of premises deriving a constraint on the concept of part.
limits: |
  This observation does not assert that the argument establishes a theory of forms; it only notes the mereological reasoning present in the passage. The conclusion about a single form is not evaluated as a metaphysical claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0209
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 159e-160a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 159e-160a
  start_marker: 159e
  end_marker: 160a
  start_char: 71401
  end_char: 72278
  text_sha256: 22b2248b17b4c8e8097fdd7faa494be97de596feb72bd1cf3d89134a1ec85d52
greek_terms:
  - τοῦ ἑνὸς πανταχῇ στέρεται
  - μετέχειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 159e-160a the argument deploys a principle that partaking of two opposite forms requires being able to partake of at least one form. Since 'the others' are deprived of the one in every way, they cannot partake of even one thing, and therefore cannot partake of two opposite forms either.
textual_basis: "At 159e the premise is stated: the others are deprived of the one in every way. The text then argues that if they were like and unlike they would have two opposite forms in themselves. The governing principle is explicitly stated: it is impossible for things to partake of two things when they do not partake of even one. At 160a the same reasoning is restated: being both like and unlike would mean partaking of two opposites, which was shown impossible."
limits: This observation records the logical structure of the argument within the Parmenides deduction. It does not assess whether the principle is valid or how it relates to other participation claims elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0210
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 159e-160a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 159e-160a
  start_marker: 159e
  end_marker: 160a
  start_char: 71401
  end_char: 72278
  text_sha256: 22b2248b17b4c8e8097fdd7faa494be97de596feb72bd1cf3d89134a1ec85d52
greek_terms:
  - οὐδὲ κινούμενα οὐδὲ ἑστῶτα
  - οὐδὲ γιγνόμενα οὐδὲ ἀπολλύμενα
  - οὐδὲ μείζω οὐδὲ ἐλάττω οὐδὲ ἴσα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 160a the deduction concludes with a rapid negative catalog: the others are not the same nor different, not moving nor at rest, not coming to be nor perishing, not greater nor smaller nor equal, nor anything else of that sort. The catalog is anchored by a single governing reason: if they underwent any such thing, they would participate in numbers (one, two, three, odd, even), which is impossible for them."
textual_basis: "At 160a, after the conclusion about likeness and unlikeness, the text launches a dense negative catalog denying sameness/difference, motion/rest, generation/destruction, greater/smaller/equal, and then states that the others undergo nothing else of that sort. This catalog is followed immediately by the unifying rationale: if the others underwent any such thing they would partake of one, two, three, odd, and even, which is impossible."
limits: This observation records the negative-catalog pattern as a closure device within a single Parmenidean deduction. It does not claim that this pattern is unique to the Parmenides or that it always functions identically elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0211
source_work: Parmenides
stephanus_span: 159e-160a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/parmenides.txt
  stephanus_span: 159e-160a
  start_marker: 159e
  end_marker: 160a
  start_char: 71401
  end_char: 72278
  text_sha256: 22b2248b17b4c8e8097fdd7faa494be97de596feb72bd1cf3d89134a1ec85d52
greek_terms:
  - ἑνὸς
  - δυοῖν
  - τριῶν
  - περιττοῦ
  - ἀρτίου
  - μεθέξει
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 160a the argument asserts that having any determinate predicate (sameness, difference, motion, rest, generation, destruction, magnitude, equality) requires participation in numbers: one, two, three, odd, even. Since the others cannot participate in numbers, the entire range of predicates is denied them in a single sweep."
textual_basis: "At 160a, after listing the predicates denied to the others, the text provides the unifying rationale: for if the others were to undergo any such thing they would partake of one and two and three and odd and even, which is impossible for them. This makes number-participation the necessary condition for any predicate attribution, collapsing a wide range of determinations under a single impossibility."
limits: This observation records the number-participation condition as it functions within this particular Parmenidean deduction. It does not claim that this principle holds across all Platonic texts or that it reflects a consistent Platonic doctrine about number and predication.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "The exchange contains five assent markers in rapid succession: 'how could it not?' (165a), 'likely' (165a), 'how so?' (165b), 'most true' (165b), and 'very much so' (165b). These responses accept the successive inferences about the appearance of equality among masses and the impossibility of grasping a beginning, end, or middle when the One does not exist."
```

```yaml

english_gloss: just as a dream in sleep appears
observation: "At 164d, the text uses a dream-in-sleep simile to characterize the illusory appearance of multiplicity: what seems smallest suddenly appears as many and enormous, like a dream in sleep."
textual_basis: "164d introduces the simile: just as a dream in sleep, suddenly instead of seeming to be one it appears many and instead of smallest, all-huge relative to the pieces broken off from it. The dream simile marks the gap between how things appear and how they are in the dialectical exploration of consequences if the One is not."
limits: This is a simile, not a reported dream narrative or a myth. It does not claim that the argument itself is a dream or that the dialectical exercise as a whole is illusory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: "164d: each mass appearing as one, but not being, if the One is not. 164e: the even and odd being in them appear not truly, if the One is not. Both formulations make the appearance/reality distinction explicit under the hypothesis that the One is not."
limits: This observation records the appearance/reality construal within a specific dialectical hypothesis (if the One is not). It does not claim that this passage asserts a theory of forms or that the construal applies outside this hypothesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 166c the speaker uses an adverbial term meaning in summary before formulating the condensed conclusion, and a perfect imperative construction meaning let this be said to mark formal closure, after running through the series of negations (neither like nor unlike, neither same nor different, etc.) that recapitulate the earlier deductions.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0217

greek_terms:
  - ἓν εἰ μὴ ἔστιν, οὐδέν ἐστιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: From the premise that without the One it is impossible to conceive of many, the summary conclusion is drawn at 166c that if the One is not, nothing is. The conclusion is presented as a correct formulation and receives the respondent's emphatic endorsement.
textual_basis: At 166b the text states that without the One it is impossible to conceive of many. From this and the chain of negations that follows, the speaker at 166c proposes the summary conclusion that if the One is not, nothing is, and asks whether this would be correctly said. The respondent assents emphatically.
limits: This observation records the structure of the concluding inference; it does not assess whether the conclusion follows from the premises or whether it is intended ironically.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_parmenides_0218

greek_terms:
  - πάντα πάντως ἐστί τε καὶ οὐκ ἔστι καὶ φαίνεταί τε καὶ οὐ φαίνεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: The dialogue closes with the comprehensive paradoxical statement at 166c that whether the One is or is not, the One itself and the others, both in relation to themselves and to each other, all things in every way both are and are not, and both appear and do not appear. The respondent characterizes this as most true.
textual_basis: The final sentence at 166c asserts universal contradictory predication across both hypotheses (the One is / the One is not), both relata (itself and others), and both relations (to themselves and to each other), encompassing both being and appearance. The respondent's final word is the superlative form meaning most true.
limits: This observation records the form of the dialogue's closure; it does not interpret whether the paradoxical conclusion is meant to be accepted, rejected, or treated as a demonstration of something beyond itself.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The formula at 165e ('let us go back once more to the beginning and say') is an explicit procedural marker. The speaker frames this as a fresh return to the hypothesis (if the One is not, what must the Others be), signaling the start of a new sub-deduction within the larger dialectical exercise.
limits: This records the procedural marker only. It does not assess the validity of the deduction that follows or its metaphysical commitments.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "The passage 165e-166a contains a rapid sequence: Parmenides proposes that the Others are not one (165e), respondent gives a short assent; then not many either, since many would contain one (165e), with assent following. The deduction moves through the premise that if the One is not in the others, they are neither many nor one (165e-166a), nor do they even appear as one or many (166a), culminating in the conclusion at 166a that no opinion or appearance of the non-existent One is possible for the Others. The respondent offers only brief confirmations at each step."
limits: This records the turn pattern and assent density. It does not evaluate the logical validity of the deductive steps.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: "The argument runs: first, there must be knowledge (160d) of it, or else one could not even know what is being said when someone says 'if the one is not' (160d). The respondent assents, and a parallel move establishes that the others must be other than it, or it could not be called other than the others. From this follows that otherness belongs to it alongside knowledge (160d-e)."
limits: This records the conditional step structure. It does not evaluate whether the inference from speakability to knowability is sound, or whether the argument presupposes disputed Parmenidean premises.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - οὔτε γίγνεται οὔτε ἀπόλλυται
english_gloss: between, neither is then nor is not, neither comes to be nor perishes
observation: At 157a, the argument claims that what changes from not-being to coming-to-be is at that moment between (metaxu) motion and rest, and in that between-state it neither is nor is not, and neither comes to be nor perishes.
textual_basis: The passage states that when something changes from not-being to coming-to-be, it is then between motions and rests (metaxu tinon tote gignetai kineseon te kai staseon), and at that time it neither is nor is not (oute esti tote oute ouk esti), neither comes to be nor perishes (oute gignetai oute apollutai). The respondent assents with 'it seems so' (eoike goun).
limits: This observation records the argument's claim about the between-state at 157a. It does not assess whether this is consistent with other Platonic treatments of change, nor does it track the term exaiphnes (the instant) which appears nearby but may not be in this exact segment.
review_status: accepted
```

